                                       CHAPTER VI


                                       THE VATICAN


           The Vatican is a sovereign state within a sovereign country,

      and it was completely in my area.        My relations with the Holy See

      ran from proper to good, to excellent.         With Pope Pius XII and

      Monsignor Montini, Acting Secretary of State, and later pope Paul

      /Vc it was proper.        With Commandatore Belardo, the civilian

      equivalent of the Secretary of State, it was proper.         With

      Monsignor McGeough and Monsignor Carroll of the American College,

      it was excellent.        And there was Monsignor O'Flaherty, the

      remarkable and incomparable man who ran the underground of

      refugees and allied prisoners of war during the Nazi occupation.

      He was portrayed by Gregory Peck in the movie, "The Scarlet and

      the Black."   I cannot put a label on my relationship with this

      amazing individual.        We were the closest and best of friends. I
7.7
      admired him for his daring and exploits under the Gestapo's nose,

      and I believe he respected me because of my methods of operation.

      Is shown in the movie, he was an excellent golfer and loved to

      play.   Two of my C.I.C. officers were good golfers, and I was the
                           4
      worst of the lot.        We played for fifty cents a hole.   O'Flaherty

      dlways won.   Many times when he made a good putt, the tall, lanky

      Irishman had to pick his money up off the green where we would

      throw it.   He would get a big kick out of it and mock us as bad

      losers.   We could only rattle him one way.        When he was ready to

      tee off, the three of us would line up stiffly, shoulder to
  shoulder and quietly and, motionless, stare at him.     He would put
  his club-down.   "So, you three poor excuses for soldiers think

  that lining up in formation can put the devil's hex on me."     Then

  he usually got off a booming shot.    He always won from us.    Then

  we would go to the C.I.C. Headquarters for a drink and dinner.

  We would always get him into a poker game and win our money back.

 He would say, "I always lose.     I suspect that you three are

 cheating me, but I can't catch you sneaky intelligence officers."

 I would tell him, "Monsignor, we wouldn't think of cheating a man

 of the cloth."    He always gave back a wisecrack.   This, in his

  best Irish brogue.

      It was a privilege and an honor to get to know and become a

 friend of the great man with a heart of gold and the courage of a

 lion.   We sat for hours discussing his underground operation.

 Jew or Gentile, white or black, soldiers of every army -- all

  were the same to him, and he snatched them from the Gestapo and

 kept them safe.     Mike Stern, an American newspaperman and head of

 the "Correspondents Association" in Rome, who had helped me get

 rid of United States communist newsmen, once asked me for the

 story on O'Flaherty's underground.    I told him, "Mike, you are a

-.good friend of mine and I am obligated to you.    I will give you

 stories, but I can't betray this confidence because it might have

 to be used again.     The communist bear is lurking in the

. background."   We had one brief encounter which, in the end,

 O'Flaherty won.   I had a system whereby I went to the

 International !Red Cross and for $200 bought an identity.    I would

 search their files for the identity of a dead or missing person

swho:fitted ona. of.my agents.   I would buy the identity and a
                       ,
        passport.    For another $100 I would get a visa from a South

        American country.    The agent assumed the identity and name.     It
        was very effective.
                                                                                    a
             The Vatican was using the same pattern to send select Nazis

        to South America, using Genoa as the port of departure.     I               a
        confronted Monsignor O'Flaherty.    He smiled and said, "I wondered

        how long it would take you to find out."   I answered, "You know I

        am going to halt this practice."   In his best Irish brogue, he
Ft,
        said, "I know you well, and I don't think you will.     I have an

        obligation to these men.    They risked their lives daily, and they

        have saved the lives of many refugees and even your American

        soldiers.    They were my informants in the Gestapo.   When the

        Gestapo found one of my hiding places, I was always warned; the

        occupants were gone.    Never was the Gestapo able to create an

        incident with the Church.    We know how you protect those who risk

        their lives for you! Do you want me to do any less?"

             I got up and said, "okay, okay.    You win again, But I will

        keep taking your money at poker even if I have to cheat.        But,

        there is one condition.    Let me know when you send them out."        He

        consented.

             An interesting note:    Monsignor O'Flaherty told me his

        greatest problem was the protest of allied officers being hidden

        together with enlisted soldiers.    Over a drink he once said, "Can

        'you imagine?   I had no room to separate them."   I laughed, and he

        said, "It wasn't funny at the time."

             My Jurisdiction extended to the Vatican walls.     One of my

        first actions' was to take down any barriers that the Germans had

        erected, and to allow free and unrestricted access to the


  ill
           Vatican.     No demonstrations were allowed.   The communist began
     11I   harassing priests and even publishing cartoons and circularlis of

           priests in bed with lovers, etc.    I put a stop to this practice

           and notified the communists that I would come down hard on them

           if it continued.

                One of the most perplexing problems with the Vatican was

           that of priests accepting missions from the Germans to cross the

           line.    We had announced that if they gave themselves up, they

           would not be held; but those who failed to turn themselves in

           were arrested.     A priest in my prison was an annoying situation.

           Each arrest necessitated a discussion with Monsignor Montini,

           acting Secretary of State.     I would go to his office in the

           Vatican.    The Swiss guards knew me, and saluted smartly as I

           would enter and drive through the gate to the left of St. Peter's

           Church and circle up through the Vatican gardens to the top

           floor.     Monsignor Montini and I would meet in a beautifully

           decorated reception room with a map of the world -on the wall

           showing the Apostolic Nuncios.     There were usually only a table

           and two chairs in the office.    The meetings were always with only

           him and me present.

                Invariably, the decision was that I would deliver the

           incarcerated priest to Church authorities.     Usually this was a

           cloistered abbey; and the agreement was that the priest could not

           leave the abbey as long as we were in Rome.     During one such
C)                The Abbott invited me to tour the abbey, and I accepted.
C)                                              •
C)         I was most impressed by one large room. Row upon row of monks

C)         were sitting on high stools, each at a high desk.     All in
C)         silence.    I asked the monk what they were doing.   He said,
CD
C)
CD
C)
C)
"Recording all the world's languages, past and present, for

posterity."

     Occasionally, I would call late at night; and it was

arranged that I would enter through a side door.    During these

meeting, I would wear civilian clothing and would meet such                0
people as Baron Von Wiezacker, the German Ambassador, Ken Harada,

the Japanese Ambassador, or other diplomats and former Italian

officials given sanctuary in the Vatican.

     Somehow, I may have violated some of their rules.    At times,

I entered wearing my gun and knife under my coat, but mostly I

remembered and left them in my car.   Monsignor O'Flaherty, the

underground and refugee leader, knew I carried weapons.     Once

sitting at a desk in his office, I took them off and placed them

on his desk and said, "Monsignor, these have become tart of my

uniform; -and at times you have to forgive me if I forget and

violate your sanctuary, but they are for your protection as well

as mine, to keep dangerous, Godless killers from your walls."

He answered, "We know that, we know what you have done and pray

for you, and thank God that he sent you here.    Someday, you will

discard these weapons and lead a long and peaceful life.       I am

proud to tell you that the Holy Father knows all about you."

(Pope Pius XII received me in three private audiences.)    I

extended my hand and said, "Thank you, Monsignor, my friend."         He

took it in both of his large hands.

     Although I had no jurisdiction within tlie Vatican walls, any

entry into the Vatican had to cross my security areas.     Any

disturbance or demonstration had to take place in'Rome, but I did

not allow any such moves against the Holy See.    In the early
                                                                                                                              Cff

                                                      days, the communists and left-wing socialists were physically


0000000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                                      attacking priests, so I put a stop to this activity and came down

                                                      hard on the perpetrators.    The ultra-left-wing newspapers began

                                                      editorials attacking priests and nuns with pornographic cartoons

                                                      and scurrilous stories. I put a quick stop to this with threats

                                                      to the editors of "Produce the evidence or go to jail."    The

                                                      President of the United States,' Special Representative to the

                                                      Vatican, the Messieurs Titman and Cowan, agreed with me; and we

                                                      became close friends.

                                                           Although not officially, technically the Vatican did fall

                                                      within my security screen.   My terms of reference and orders did

                                                      not include the Vatican; but my men and I, with tacit consent of

                                                      my General, assumed responsibility for their security, because

                                                      acts against the Vatican could affect our safety.

                                                           The Secretariat of State had a civilian equivalent to

                                                      Monsignor Montini; his name was Commendatore Belardo.    He was in

                                                     Th is sixties, and was the continuity when changes occurred in the

                                                      position of Secretary of State.    After my visits began, Belardo

                                                      would invariably ask me if I wanted to tour certain areas of the
                                              4,4
                                                      Vatican.   For example, one whole afternoon was taken in.a private

                                                    , tour and explanation of the Vatican treasures.   He was an expert

                                                      and explained the details to me.   These tours and explanations

                                                      went on for yearh.   After one session, Belardo said, "Senor

                                                      torso, workmen were repairing a wall, and they discovered a

                                                      Painting behind the outer wall done on an inner wall.     No

                                                      outsider has seen it; you will be the first if you so desire."      I

                                                      said, "Please, may I see it?" It was a beautiful painting.

                                                      Belardo said, "We believe it to be done by one of the great
          masters, possibly Cellini or Michelangelo.     It will be studied

          before we announce this great discovery."

I]             "Pieta," Michelanglo's great statue, had been walled in to

          protect it from any bombing.    The day it was uncovered, I was

          invited to the unveiling or de-walling.     At every special

          function, I was always invited and sat in the diplomatic section.

               The Vatican treasure tours were most interesting, but for me

          the highlight was the tour of the Sistine Chapel and Belardo's
15        explanation of each fresco.    Cellini's mosaics also were

          fascinating.   They even researched my family tree and gave me my

          family coat of arms.   I felt many times that I was a most

          fortunate individual to be put in a position to be treated as an

          honored guest and friend.    Perhaps they recognized that through

          me they would be better understood.     Upon my return to the

          Pentagon in 1947, I wrote the only report that had ever been

          compiled by intelligence and placed in the Pentagon reference

          files.

               Often during conferences, the Monsignors and Bishops would

          look at each other and smile when I would throw back.at them

         .words and phrases they had used months before.    After all, they

         3 were experts in the mind.- thought process.   I think they knew me

          better than I knew myself.

               However, everything was- not always rosy or buddy - buddy.      I

          once had a heated discussion with Monsignor Montini (later Pope

          Paul VI) and acting Secretary of State, over two Gestapo officers.

         'hiding'in the Vatican.   Although he was noncommittal.   I did get

     .    an anonymous phone call on my personal phone, telling when they

         • were leaving the Vatican.    Many discussions centered around the
00000000000
                                          operations of the Vatican Bank, especially in dealings in United

                                    2     States Postal Money Orders and script.      I don't think it ever

                                          stopped, but it was curtailed.

                                               But, we had two irritants which were never settled or agreed

                                          upon.    One was when I traced some false intelligence along, with

                                          maps, to the Vatican that the Yugoslays were going to invade

                                          Italy.    I told them that even if their purpose was to make us

                                          aware of the communist danger to Italy, their method and approach


 00000000000000000000000000000000
                                          was so unprofessional that, in a way, it was an insult to my

                                          Intelligence.    (I never saw any feeling of remorse or received an

                                          apology.)

                                               The second irritant was the Vatican newspaper, Osservatore

                                          Romano.    I told them I was having enough problems with the

                                          irresponsible Italian press and the editorials of the communist

                                          papers which could lead to anarchy, and some of their stories and

                                          editorials could well have been written by the Unita communist

                                          editors.     Visibly, they didn't like this comparison.

                                               The French had Black African soldiers whom we knew as

                                          "Goums."     They were considered just about the best "assault"

                                          troops in the world.    They were fierce fighters and swept

                                        ... everyone before them, though they did not differentiate between

                                          enemy troops and.civilians, and took few prisoners.       Once they
                                                             4
                                          took a position, they would not hold it and had to be pulled from

                                          the lines.    They were the best African troops.   The French

                                          officers wore the same robe.    Discipline was. severely strict.

                                          Disobeying ,an order meant instant death.    The French officers

                                          would shoot the soldier on the spot.    They had their own

                                          concubines who were kept in locked, barred vans.     Every so often
                                                                               ,0

       we would get circulerds that Fatima so and so had escaped, was

       diseased and should be approached and returned to the French

       headquarters.    While on the attack, their motto was "Kill!    Kill!
      Kill!", often accompanied by excesses on the civilian population.

       This often happened during an attack.

            The British had the highly - trained, deadly "Gurkhas."

      They had to comb their long hair every evening.    They carried, it

      was said, five curved knives in the tail of their tunic.    Once,

       while visiting a British camp, a "Gurkha" was on guard.    AS I

      approached, the guard reached back and drew a knife.     I went for

       my pistol.   'My British escort began to laugh as the "Gurkha"

       brought the knife up vertically before his face in a salute.      I

       was embarrassed as I returned the salute.    These remarkable

      troops used to go on patrols for a week or ten days at a time

       behind German Eines.   They struck terror in the hearts of the

      Germans, and they roamed only at night.    To prove they had done a

       good job, they brought back ears and noses.
:J1

 L.
            The Osservatore Romano lumped them with the "Goums" as

      savages loosed on the countryside and an unsuspecting Italian

      people.   I protested in writing and in face - to - face meetings.

      One such encounter became quite heated when one of the Monsignors

      said these trot:gig were guilty of atrocities and that the printed

      storieS were true.    I answered angrily, "Atrocities, you say? I

      :have seen these troops attack valiantly in the face of heavy

      German fire.     I have seen them strewn on the battlefield, dead,

       wounded, gnd blown to bits by German artillery fire and mortars.

      They died to' rid Italy of the Nazis.    They didn't ask to come

       here, but they fought and died for what you believe.    Yes, some
0000000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                                      of the stories are true, but why don't you print that I saw

                                                      American soldiers tied to tanks of the attacking Hermann Goering

                                                      S.S. Panyzer Division?     Our soldiers for weeks after this refused

                                                      to take prisoners.    Those boys, tied to those tanks, didn't ask

                                                      to come here and die.     Why don't you print that story that we

                                                      took no prisoners and inflame the people against the Americans?

                                                     I am not so much interested in the content of the story, but what

                                                     it might cause.     Confiscating your paper will only highlight the

                                                     situation.     I am faced with a most volatile situation at the

                                                     moment.    The communists are quick to take advantage of situations

                                                      that you are helping to create.      They are picking up your

                                                     stories.    There is talk of a revolution.      They have arms stored

                                                      everywhere.     If they move before I do, there will be bloodshed

                                                      and destruction.    Your priests, nuns and installations will be

                                                     caught in such a revolution.      Many of my soldiers, Italian

                                                     police, Italian Army and civilians will die.        Their deaths may

                                                      not be on your conscience, but they will be on mine; because I

                                                     did not take every measure to stop any incitement which could

                                                     lead to riots and killing."      I certainly laid it all on the line.

                                                     But why not?     That's the way I felt.
                                              •fl
                                                           Perhaps I overreacted, but I was angry and possibly rude.           I

                                                     got up, turned rig back and walked out of the room.        There was a

                                                      deadly silence.

                                                    :       A few days later, with the help of the Chief of Police of

                                                     I struck bard.     We captured or destroyed all the hidden arms,

                                                     neutralized the arsenals, arrested the perpetrators and

       revolution abruptly came to a halt, and untiltI left:Italy there

       was no more talk— of a violent communist—takeover.of—the

       government.

            Shortly after, I received a call from the American College

       inviting me to dinner.    The table setting was most elegant,

       crystal glasses, fine china and beautifully designed silverware.

       The Bishop sat at the head of the table.     My two American

       friends, Monsignor McGeough and Monsignor Carroll, were present

       with other dignitaries.    The food would have done justice to any

       of the world's five star restaurants.     The wine was the finest

       and the after dinner drinks were the best.     I clearly saw that

       this was a fence — mending invitation.     I played the part and

       clearly let it be understood that I was among friends and with

       people who I greatly respected and admired.     A few days later, I

       received a notice from the Head of the Ceremonial that the Holy

       Father wished to receive me in private audience.    This was the

       third time.     Pope Pius XII was most gracious and again asked

       about my family.   This time he blessed my family and my wife and
                                                                   o' i
       said we would have a long and full life together. (Forty fi-vc
                        weaL
L1     years lat—er we a3'e-94*n happily married.)

            The next day I received a call from Colonel Gene Morena, the

       head of the C.I.C.    He asked me to come to dinner at the

       headquarters.    When I arrived, Monsignor O'Flaherty was there.

     ' He had brought bottles of a good vintage wine and proposed      "A

       toast to his American friends, especially their leader."       Later,

       when we were leaving, the Monsignor left with me.    I said,

       "Monsignoi-,.I am sorry I got angry and crude, and bad mannered

       with your people."     He answered, "Filippo, (he had never called
000000000
                                      "Ti
                                             me by my first name) you neverlhave asked anything for yourself;

                                             and when it comes to the welfare and lives of your men, and even

                                             us in the Church, you are uncompromising even if your life is at

                                             stake.     For this reason, you have won the respect of us all,

                                             including the Holy Father."    I was most proud to hear these

                                             words.     I fought our enemies with the gun and knife; he used his


 0000000000000000000000000000000000
                                             courage and faith to defeat them.    I was bursting with pride to

                                             hear these words from a man who I considered the bravest of the

                                             brave.

                                                  As we were getting in our cars, he said, "God has chosen you

                                             for a long and fruitful life.    Your country will be proud of

                                             you."    I looked up at him and I don't know why I said,

                                             "Monsignor, if I am a chosen one, why won't God let me beat you

                                             at the game of golf?"    He stopped, began to laugh; I think I saw

                                             a tear in his eye, and this great man without saying a word, came

                                             over and embraced me like I was a small child.

                                                  I began to receive letters from the Holy See on Vatican

                                             stationary which read:

                                                         The Holy Father in his mission of mercy wishes to
                                                         intercede for --- (followed by prisoner's name)

                                                  These letters were extremely disturbing.      I asked for a

                                             conference.    It was granted at the American College at the

                                             Vatican.    Present were my two friends, Monsignor McGeough and

                                             Monsignor Carrol.    4..Bishop presided.   In my best and supposedly

                                             most eloquent manner., I began (I placed the letters on the

                                             table),    "I allow a priest of your choosing to go into my prison

                                             to administer to the spiritual needs of the prisoners.      These

                                            • .letters are an obvious violation of a trust.    I should arrest

                                             your prieSt--since he is jeopardizing the lives of my people.
                                                                        0 78

      have violent and dangerous agents in the prisons.     Often, I have
      caught only one or two members of a sabotage ring.     I cannot
      chance that it becomes known to the others, not in custody, that

      I have caught some of them.   The others might flee or commit acts

      which could result in many deaths.   It appears that your priest's

      actions are governed by his heart and not his head.     If it

      doesn't stop, I will lock him up incommunicado with the others."

      The Bishop, at this point, interceded and said, "We didn't

      realize, but you have my personal word of honor, it will stop."

           I was then invited to dine with them.     At the table, I told

L 1   the Bishop,that I wished to discuss the letters; I continued, "I

      wish to assure you that if a prisoner is guilty, no pressure or

      intercession will get him released; however, if a man is

      innocent, I give you my word, I will release him immediately.

      You know how I treat my prisoners, at times harsh, at time

      perhaps you wouldn't approve.   But I have to keep diseipline,

      since I am often dealing with the dregs of humanity.     Ldon't

      like it, but so it is.   We do not deal in torture or inhumane

      methods.   I promise you this will continue.   This promise is

      coupled with my word and personal honor while 1 am in command."

      The Bishop looked at me and said, "Corso, we know this; and your

      friends here, •Monsignors' McGeough and Carroll, swear by you and

      would stake their lives on your word. Now, let's enjoy our

      dinner, and we will all toast our honored guest."

           I must say, and I would be lying if I didn't add that I was

      flattered.

           On a trip to Sicily, where I escorted some top Vatican Nazi

      diplomats accredited to the Vatican, to Taormina for internment,
0000000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                              I was accompanied.by Monsignor McGeough.     To show how little I

                                              knew of what motivated these men, I asked the Monsignor, '"When

                                              are you going to get promoted to Bishop?"    (I was thinking like a

                                              military officer.)

                                                     His answer was, "On no, I never want to be a Bishop and be

                                              responsible for so many more souls."    I was embarrassed to think,

                                              I know so little about how such men live and think, and he must

                                              have thought what a stupid question from a supposedly intelligent

                                              man.

                                                     As time passed, my faux pas must have been overlooked

                                              because soon after the war, the Vatican convened their first

                                              consistory in many years, in order to make new Cardinals.      I was

                                              invited as an honored guest.    At the reception, I was told to

                                              await for Monsignor McGeough, who was the Chief organizer.        When

                                              he arrived, he asked me to wait as he wanted to take me through

                                              the reception line of the new American Cardinals. The first in

                                              line was the new and great Cardinal Spellman of New York.      To my

                                              great surprise    he took me in as - the first guest and introduced

                                              me to Cardinal Spellman with these words, "Your Eminence, this is

                                              the American I spoke to you about.    He has done more for the

                                              Church in these troubled times than any other man in Italy."        I

                                              think for the .first time in my life I was speechless.    I did

                                              nothing special, only my job; but they must have thought

                                              otherwise.

                                                     With this introduction, began .a lifetime of friendship with

                                              his Eminence Cardinal Spellman.    During my tour on the White

                                              House Staff, I had the privilege of the counsel of this wise man

                                              on a constant basis.    Until this writing, I have never revealed
        this fact.   Monsignor Montini, later Pope Paul VI, was Acting

        Secretary of State; because the position was being held for

        Cardinal Spellman, who never took the position.    He, along with

        high Vatican officials, decided he would be more valuable to the

        Church in New York, and he possibly gave up his chance to become

        Pope.   Remembering my question and answer from Monsignor McGeough

        during our many meetings, I never dared to ask Cardinal Spellman

        the same question of becoming Pope.   What answer would he have

        given me?"   that he "Didn't want to be responsible for so many

        more souls."    I will never know.

             Three times during my stay in Rome, I was invited to private

        audiences with Pope Pius XII.   On each occasion I would be
l;f1.

        ushered into a large room.   The light shown through sheer white .

        silk drapes.    The Pope would enter; I would kiss his ring and he,

        in English, would inquire about my family.    He would then thank

        me for coming and depart.

             I asked my liaison officer, "Why does he send for me and

        then we go through the same thing each time?"     My liaison

        officer, Captain Mario Bolaffio, would then say. "Phil, don't you

        realize what an honor he is paying you?   People from all over the

        world, even the high and mighty, would give anything for such an

        audience alone, just you, and the Holy Father. It is his way

        of recognizing and thanking you for something you have done.

            I thought, am I no more than a 28 year old, stupid hick from

        a small town?    When I think back, how could I have been so

        unsophisticated and ignorant?    An honor few people have the

        opportunity to live through, and I didn't know any better.

             Pope Pius XII was attacked for supposedly his lack of action
                                                                                ,,0


      in the Ardentine massacre case.    Some went so far as to blame

      him.    This was a scurrilous attack on a wonderful man.   I     •

      investigated the massacre.     An Italian street sweeper had a

      powerful bomb in his cart and set it off as a company of marching

      German soldiers approached.    Thirty-two Germans were killed.

      Under international law, the Germans could take, for such events,

      hostages ten to one, which they did and executed the 320 innocent

      civilians who had no part in the deed.    In my investigation, I

     could find no military value tn this action except to‘pfgovoke the

     situation leading to more killings.     I found that the order for

      the bombing cape from out of the country, and was given by

     Ercole-Ercole, the' pseudonym for Palmiro Togliatti, the head of

      the Italian Communist Party.    The blame for the massacre, too,

     should have been laid on his head.

             Years later, perhaps I repaid Pope Pius XII.   The play "The

     Deputy" was laying the blame on him.
C)           Hochhuth's play was well received in most West European
C)
     countries, primarily because it revived interest in an old World
C)
C)   War II issue.    It was not nearly so well received in the United

     States, though there continues to quite an appetite for anything
CD   irelated to Hitler's "Holocaust."    Careful reading of "The Deputy"
C)
C)   does not fully resolve the issue of Papal responsibility for the

C)   deaths of many thousands.     I got tired of the distortions and

     lles, and wrote a long letter to a New York newspaper, which they

     published.    In this letter I said that since I officially
C)
C)   investigated the Ardentine Cave massacre, and I never saw any of

C)   the writers or, directors of the play on my investigative team,
C)   their play was a fabrication, full of lies and distortions.           If
r-     they questioned my word, I challenged them to come forward or lay

       the blame where it rightfully belonged -- on the communists.

      Naturally, no one ever came forward to challenge me.    The
       newspaper stories died down; perhaps Pope Pius XII forgave me for

      my lack of appreciation when he paid me the great honor of

      receiving me in private audience.

            In the mid — 1940's, the Vatican had plans to move to

      Argentina if the communist took over Italy.    I like to believe

      that I upset the communist plans; perhaps this is what Monsignor

      McGeough meant when he said, "I had done more than any other man

      in Italy for the Church."

            Within my limited experience, there is one thing I learned

      during this period.    The Pope has been criticized many times for

      not doing more about the Holocaust.    These accusations puzzled

      me.   What was apparent to me each time I went to the Vatican was

      that the Swiss guards, in their Middle Age costumes, saluted me

      with spears.   The deadly enemy outside the Vatican walls was

      composed of killers and terrorist •armed with the best weapons

      known to the world.    The Pope had only ideological weapons.      I

      was -warned early to fight fire with fire.   The Gestapo, I

     .. -learned, was waiting for an excuse to invade the Vatican.   I

      destroyed the remnants of this deadly menace with weapons and

      fire.   As I was tbld by Chief Ferrari. "Treat them nice, they

      will blow -your head off."   The Jews and other refugees in the

      Vatican remainedisafe.    Contrary to the Biblical saying, "Turn

      ,the other cheek,"   I struck first, then thought about it.    The

      result of our combined efforts, the Vatican is still here in

      Rome; the refugees survived; Rome is a thriving free city; and I
       am still alive. The same applied to the communist menace once
                                                               •
      the Gestapo was gone.. I struck at them, destroyed their plans

      for the takeover of the Italian Government, and stopped NKVD•

      (KGB) agent provocateurs from operating in Rome.

              While paying a visit to the Secretariat of State at the

       Vatican I mentioned that I had information that Tito's communist

       partisans were killing and persecuting priests and seminarians
I]
       and it was going to get worse.      Tito's number two man was running

       the show and he has direct ties to Moscow.      I concluded, "I hate

       to think what will happen to your priest in Yugoslavia if Moshe

       Pijade becomes 21eputy Minister in a Yugoslav communist

     .—'4overnment. • Their methods do not differ much from that of the

       Nazi's,    Religion or human life Means nothing to them.'    The

       ardwer.: got, "We will have to pray that they     ill change."

       answered, 'Monsignor, perhaps over a long period of time your

       method will work, but I don't have time to wait for the devil's

1i     spawn to evolve into decent human beings.     I have to take them at

        face value, and crush them before they destroy me and my

        soldiers.   I am sorry, but I will not turn the other cheek, I

        have been taught to not even let them slap the first cheek.           Mine

        is not a holy war, but a deadly game of kill or be killed.        I

        didn't ask to be here, but here I am, responsible for the

        security of mine and yours."      The Monsignor asked, "How old are

        you?"    I replied,   "28".   I think that was the first time I saw

        an expression of respect or pity, I didn't know which, on his

        face.    Little did I know I was talking to the future Pope Paul

        VI.

                                                       will the Vatican speak up?"                                      He was very candid in his answer,

                                                      "We have received word f                                   m our suffering brethren, not to

                                                      ,protest their treatment because a papal provocation only

                                                      intensifies atrocities and ill treatment."                                                  I answered, "Then the

                                                      only answer is that we destroy them by the force of arms."                                                                   He

                                                      said, "I am afraid so, you and yours are God's swords."                                                                  My reply

                                                      was, "Monsignor, I don't feel like God's sword, but, I assure you

                                                      that any Gestapo, S.S. or NKVD (KGB) that comes in my area will

                                                       not last long, and if he resists, he will not be long for this

                                                      world."

                                                           The Meeting ended and he gave me his blessing.

                                    and was in the Vatican, where I met him, told me that Pope Pius

                                    XII was in secret contact with them not to heed Gestapo demands,

                                    to turn the Jews over to Nazi control.      He said that they had to

                                    be careful and make the delayed responses to the Gestapo not an

                                    obvious rebuke, but make it appear as ineptness and inefficiency.

                                    They feared .direct pressure from. Aitler on Mussolini, but.,

                                    fortunately, this never came about-.


 0000000000000000000000000000000
                                         During one of my visits to this official, he said he was

                                    going to lead a new fascist organization to fight the communist.

                                    I told. him, "With what, you have no arms." . Make any disturbances

                                    in Rome and I will lock up all of your followers.     He never did.

                                         Shortly after I met with Vatican officials and they

                                    confirmed his story.   They told me that he had been relieved

                                    after being accused of homosexuality, which they said was not so,

                                    but he was attracted to young girls.    He had asked for one and

                                   .used the word "Maschietto," which could be translated "Tom Boy."

                                        :Pope Pius XII and Vatican officials had decided that open

                                    provocation had to be avoided. Publicity could only lead to

                                    possible irresistible pressure on Mussolini, if Hitler had been

                                    prodded by open dialogue.   I was told that a Rabbi Wise had asked

                                    the Vatican to influence 'Mussolini.   This meant little to me

                                   since. I did not know anything about Rabbi Wise.     I never inquired

                                    into.any more details of the background of the story, but the end

                                    result was that none were turned over to the Gestapo.     My defense

                                                                           THE JEWISH COMMUNITY


0000000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                                        Soon after I became the Rome Area Command Chief of

                                                   Intelligence and Security, I met with the elders of the Jewish

                                                   community.   I assured them that they would not be harassed, and

                                                   the days of any persecution were over.    They were free to live

                                                   and worship as they saw fit.   If they wished to do so and knew of

                                                   any Gestapo stay behinds, please let me know; and I would cleanse

                                              Ii   Rome of them.   I would let it be known that I would not condone

                                                   any activity or action against them, and they would be treated as

                                                   any other pers6ns under my protection.    In case of any trouble, I


                                               I   would dispatch armed men for their protection.    I never had to

                                                   send one armed man and found out that this was a sophisticated

                                                   and high - class community also respected by most Italians.        Now

                                                   that the Fascists and Nazis were gone, the only threat I could

                                                   see was from neo-fascist or communists.

                                                        They, each in turn, thanked me for my concern and attitude.

                                                   I told them it was more than that; it was my duty.   They repaid

                                                   me many times over during the next four years for my attitude and

                                                   security measures.

                                                        On one occasion,   General Dasher called me; and he gave me a

                                                   telegram which said that Hermann Goering's art expert and buyer

                                                   was hiding in Rome.   The telegram didn't even have his name.     The

                                                   General said, "Do the best you can."   I called the head of the

                                                   Jewish community and asked if they could find him for me.   He

                                                   said, "I will let you know."   Within three hours he called and

                                                   had his name and the address of his hideout.   I dispatched a

                                                   C.I.C. team to arrest him.   I walked into General Dasher's office
        that same afternoon and asked, "I have him, General; what do I do

        .with him?"   The surprised General said, "So soon?   How in the
        hell did you do it?" 'I answered, "I have friends."    The General
        then said, "I don't know what to do with him; hold him until I

        get instructions."    We were ordered to send him to the Allied

        Command in Germany.

             On the outskirts of Rome there was a refugee camp at "Cin'e

7   1   Citta"   (Movie City).   The camp was not a pleasant place, and

        most of the people lived almost in squalor.    The Italian

        authorities and even the allies wanted to close it down.     But the

        persistent problem was what to do with the refugees, most    of

        whom were Jews.

             The head of the Jewish community and an Assistant Minister

        named Santini in charge of refugees at the Ministry of Interior,

        came to see me.   They were both friends of mine; and when they

        came in, I said, "I must be in trouble.    What have I done or not

        done?"

             The Minister spoke up and said, "You know the conditions at

        Cin'e Citta since we have been there before together, and we are

        getting ready to move a large contingent out of the camp."

             The head of the Jewish community then took up the

        conversation.   "We have ships waiting at Taranto in Southern

        Italy to move 10,000 Jewish refugees to Israel.    All arrangements

        have been made except one."

             "I see," I said, "And that one is me."   "Yes, you will find

        Out and can *stop us."   I continued, "You already made your plans

        on the assumption I would support you."   Santini said, "The move
0 0 00000000000
                                   13


                                         has the blessings of Prime Minister De Gaspari and the Holy is;ae."

                                         I added, "You realize I am the Allied G-2; and if I keep this

                                         from the British, who will certainly object to the move because

                                         they are fighting the Jews in Palestine, I might not be in this

                                         position too long."    Santini said, "We fully realize your

                                         position and have no choice but to ask your help."

                                              I thought for awhile and looked at the picture on my desk of

                                         my little boy, whom I had never seen, and then answered them,

                                         "What the hell, I have been here too long anyway.    Let them send

                                         me home.   Here is what I will do for you since, I suppose, the


  000000000000000000000000000000
                                                     /ui
                                         future of -101:0Q0-people is of utmost importance; and I am sure

                                         you both feel that way:    I will send you a military adviser to

                                         teach you how to move a large convoy.   To move 10,000 people, the

                                         convoy will be too long.   Split it in five sections.   I think

                                   77,
                                         that I can give you about three truckloads of food and some

                                         gasoline and some medical supplies.   I will furnish no military

                                         transportation since the move has to appear as a civilian

                                         operation.   Santini, tell Chief Ferrari to contact Moro for me,

                                         and I want five icelere' jeeps with two armed police each.    They

                                         will go to my C.I.C. headquarters and join five of my C.I.C.

                                         jeeps.   One police and one C.I.C. jeep, with passes from me, will

                                         stay with each unit.   The last big city you will pass through

                                         will be Naples. I will alert the C.I.C. Commander in Naples to

                                         insure safe passage.   I urge utmost secrecy, and you must begin

                                         to move in three days."

                                             The stately, graying, head of the Jewish community took my

                                         hand and said, "We will never forget this."   I answered, "I don't

                                         know if I am doing those people a favor; the men will be pitted
      against trained armies.                                         I hope they will survive."

              They arrived safely in Israel; what happened after
                                                                 that, I
      don't know.               The similarities of this operation to that
                                                                           written
      by novelist Leon (iris in Exodus                                                      are quite evident.                        Obviously,
17:   it was not in my particular interest to follow this
                                                          "exodus" all
      the way to Israel which, at that time,. did not actually
                                                               exist. My
      direct responsibility ended the moment those trucks left the Rome

      area; and, technically, my escort jeeps and men had no authority,

      beyond my verbal orders, to protect these -4TIMIXO- Jewish refugees;

                Now, the agonizing task of notifying my Commanding General

          of the decision I had made.    He was the type of man that I could

          not take such an action behind his back.    Fortunately, he knew
          that the nature of my business was that I had to make many

          decisions on the spur of the moment.   I always felt that his.

          explicit trust in me was a decided plus.   I went to his office

          and I said, "General, today I made a decision and started an

          operation which may have repercussions on me and possibly spill

          over on you.   You can order me to fill you in, but I would rather

          you wouldn't."    He could sense my concern, and the understanding

          gentleman said, "What's new?   Haven't you always operated in this
rry
          manner?   That's why you are one of my top staff officers to act        0
          in our best interest, and I never required you to clear every

          action with me.    When you are ready to fill me in, come back.    In

          the meantime, go about your business."   As I was leaving, he

          added, "This must be a good one, since you are concerned about

          me.   I will be anxious to hear your story."

                I thought, thank God, I have such a Commanding General over


      (   me.   Later, the repercussions came, but were disguised and aimed
                                        primarily at my dismissal.


0000000000000000
                                            Shortly after the episode of the Jewish refugees, a bomb

                                       exploded in the entrance of the British Embassy in Rome.      The

                                       Italian police and military intelligence moved immediately on the

                                       case and brought me pieces of the explosive missile and arrested

                                       two of the perpetrators; both were Jewish.    I called the heads of

                                       the Jewish community and told them I wanted to meet with them

                                       immediately.   I was angry and said, '"If you want to bomb.

                                 V.    embassies and legatiom, go to Naples, Milan, or even Paris.     I

                                       thoUght you were my friends.. flu know I have to investigate these

                                       cases and arrept the guilty ones."     Just before the meeting, I

                                       was hit with another bomb.    In the raid the Italians had picked

                                       up at the apartment of the perpetrators, the organization and

                                       names of the Irgum Zei Leumi and the Shrn gang.


   000000000000000000000000000
                                            I got up and paced the room.    I was irate.   I said to the

                                       Jewish representatives, "I know you were governed by emotion, but

                                       be realistic; for a penny's worth of satisfaction, you may have

                                 ii    icaused many, many lives to be lost.   Do you realize that if these

                                       lists fall into communist hands, and Togliatti is Minister of

                                       Grace and Justice, how many of your people may be killed --
                                 rmi   innocent brave men may die because of an error in judgement.        I

                                       am not even considering the loss if they fall into British

                                       hands."

                                            Without any further ado, I went to the telephone, threw all

                                       caution to the wind and dialed Prime Minister De Gasperi's

                                       number.   I reached the private secretary and told him what I

                                  ^


                                       wanted, and that this was matter of utmost importance.     Within

                                       ten minutes I received a call that a judge, who had the case and
     did not like Togliatti, was waiting with the papers.   I called
     the C.I.C. and told them to pick up the judge and bring him to

     the Club 57, a Night Club Restaurant.   They arranged a beautiful

     meal for the judge as my guest.

          I later called the head of the Jewish elders and told him I

     had everything safe in my possession, including the two

     perpetrators.
11       I could hear him breathe a sigh of relief.    Then he asked me

     if I would honor him and his family as a guest at his son's Bar

     Mitzvah, when a Jewish boy announces to all the guest and family

     in a beautiful ceremony that he is entering adulthood and becomes

     a man.   I accepted the invitation, and I was treated as a member

     of the family.   This was the first time I entered a synagogue and

     wore the little skull cap.   I thought, perhaps I had a little to

     do with preserving this place of worship.
