                                    CHAPTER XII


                              C. I. G. (NOW C. I. A.)                             0
I]
                                                                                  0
                            CENTRAL INTELLIGENCE GROUP
                                                                                  0
                                                                                  0
           The SSU—CIG was the successor of OSS and was now under the             0
      Department of State.     Later C.I.G. became C.I.A.   Because of so
                                                                                  0
      many bungled and questionable operations, I never had any use for

      OSS during combat military intelligence days in Italy.                      0
      Especially when they sent thousands of German rifles to

      Mihalovich, communist Tito's enemy, ih Yugoslavia; and the rifles
                                                                                  0
      arrived without bolts and were useless.     I had no use for C.I.G.
                                                                                  0
      in Rome, and later, in Korea and the White House, I had no use

      for C.I.A.     I formed the opinion through experience that they

      thought they were a free wheeling elite with too much money and
                                                                                  0
      answerable only to themselves.    They covered their miserable
                                                                                  0
      mistakes and ideology with a "Top Secret" stamp.      Never in my           0

      many years of combat in intelligence and in high — level staff
                                                                                  0
      positions, did I know of one action where OSS, C.I.G. or C.I.A.
                                                                                  0
      enhanced our war effort.    I make this statement without
                                                                                  0
      reservation, since I had all the clearance and had access to such           0

      information.    The head of C.I.G. in Rome was James Angleton, who          0
                                                                                  0
     I dater became head of C.I.A.'s Counter intelligence Division in
                                                                                  0
      Washington.    My relationship with him in Rome was, at its best,           0
      strained to outright animosity; and no love was lost between us.

      Rome was under military occupation.     As the top intelligence

      officer in the area, no operation was allowed without my


Li
                                                                                                                                 I


0 000000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                                        permission.

                                                             In the forty odd years of the existence of the C.I.A,, Jim

                                                        Angleton was probably the least loved and most feared man at or

                                                        near the top of that vast agency.    I never did figure out where

                                                        his influence came from, although I had suspicions, wherever it

                                                        came from, I didn't care and challenged him at every turn.       There
                                                        were many who tolerated him, though their feelings' were probably,

                                                        at best, acquiescence rather than real feeling.     And, despite the

                                                        unwieldy bureaucracy, C.I.A. was and remains, Angelton had the

                                                        power to make disapproved underlings suffer his basic cruelty.
                                               if
                                                        In this, he was, probably more a C.I.A. type man than most of
                                               i
                                                        those pseudo — romantic males who play C.I.A. roles on
                                                   Ei
                                                        television.

                                                             As already maintained, I first crossed paths (and swords)

                                                        with Angleton first in Rome where he was the Chief of C.T.G.

                                                        (Central Intelligence Group, later to become the Central

                                                        Intelligence Agency.)

                                                             A few weeks after the British Embassy bombing episode,

                                                        Angelton came to my office, virtually pleading with me to turn

                                                        over the papers of the two Jewish organizations to him. 4-4 1-

                                                        eeteed—thistin—deLail—t.in-m prevtoun ahopkercp)   I refused.    I

                                                        don't know how he ,found out; I'm sure the Italians didn't tell

                                                        him, because they had no use for him.   After a process of

                                                        elimination, I surmised it must have been Israeli Intelligence.

                                                        He got angry when I turned him down, and he insisted I had to

                                                        give them to him. T answered, "Do I?. Who says so, you, or your

                                                        British Foreign Office friends who have been visiting you?       You

                                                        sure wined and dined them."   (One of his visitors was Kim Philby,
      who later was found out to be a Soviet spy.)

           About ten days later, General Dasher called me in and . showed

      me an order from Washington which read that he turn over the

      papers to Angleton.   I told the General, "I don't like this."'

      The General answered, "Neither do I, but I have no choice."     He

411   added, "You don't like Angleton?"   I said, "No, General, my
      instinct tells me not to trust him; and like the Indians say, and

      I have the proof, 'he speaks with a forked tongue'."    I delivered
      the Papers and threatening, I said, "Heaven help you, Jim, if I

      ever find out that these papers ever got in the wrong hands

      instead of going to Washington.   I made a promise to these

      people." (Perhaps Israeli intelligence can answer these

      questions:   Did he send them to Washington?    Did he give them tb

      the British (Philby) and so to the communists?     Or did he use

      them to ingratiate himself with the Israelis?    Perhaps the answer

      lies with trees recently planted in Israel in his behalf.)


           In a recent book, Spycatcher, by Peter Wright, who spent two

      decades in the top echelons of 1115, the British Security SerVice,

         , wrote, "... I knew from bitter experience that Angleton was
      •W.#.

      more than capable of manufacturing evidence when none existed."

           He tried the same tactic on me in Rome in 1945.

           One night, about 10 p.m., I received a desperate phone call

      from Angleton.   Be sounded almost panicky.    He said that he had

      two Germans at his place in Rome.   They went out for a walk, and

      one was recognized by his ex -chauffeur.   The Italian police

      arrested them. I asked the Chief of Police of Italy, Ferrari,
                 •
      but he said he would only release them in your custody.

           I answered him, "What Were you up to with them?     You never.
0000p000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                                cleared holding them with me."

                                                     Angleton replied, "I can't tell you, but it is of utmost

                                                importance to the United States."

                                                     In sarcasm, I shot back, "Excuse me, Jim, I will stand up

                                                and salute."

                                                     In his panicky voice he said, "I am serious.    You have to

                                                get them from the Italians as soon us possible."    I was surprised

                                                when Chief Ferrari- came on the phone.   Angleton was in his

                                                office, and he knew that I had warned him against dealing

                                                directly with the police.   I told Ferrari, "Get them ready,

                                                Excellency, and I will send my men to pick them up tonight.    Tell

                                                Angleton I am taking custody."   I could almost hear him chuckle,

                                               since he had no use for Angelton; and he knew I would make him

                                                sweat.

                                                    I made up my mind that no matter who the prisoners were, I

                                                would not turn them over to Ahgleton.

                                                  ' The next day, Angleton was in my office bright and early.      I

                                               told him, "Damn you, I told you never to interfere with the

                                               police.   I have no prisoners by the names you gave me in my

                                               security prison.   Now, excuse me, the General wants to see me."

                                               As I was leaving, I said, "Contact Washington and tell them I am

                                               obstructing you; .see if you can get me fired!"

                                                    The Germans were not in my security prison, but in two

                                               'special cells in the United States Military Police Jail.    One was

                                               a high ranking diplomat in the former German Embassy in Rome, and

                                               the other was a German Lieutenant General, Chief of Staff to

                                               General Wolff, who had surrendered the German armies in Northern

                                               Italy.
               They vere both tight-lipped and wouldn't talk.    I told my
         C.1.C., "Let them simmer for awhile, then we will tackle them all

         in about ten days.    I did get an inquiry from the British Foreign

         Office and told them I had no prisoners by those names, and with

         malice I told them, "Ask Angleton."

               In about a week, I received a message from the two prisoners
    RI
         that unless I released them, documents were going to be released

         which would embarrass the. United States for the role played in

         North Africa.    The deadline was the next day at 6:00 p.m.   I
         called Pagnotta. at the C.I.C. and told him I wanted him to

-   C    accompany me, to the prison the next day and bring six of the

         Custom guards.

               At 5:30 p.m. we sat in the Provost Marshall's Office-       I

         sat at the desk; Leo sat alongside me.    I. had the prisoners

         brought up.   I didn't give them the dignity of even a greeting.

         They were left standing, while Leo and I disregarded them and

         joked about trivialities.   Finally, at 20 minutes before six, I

         turned to them and said, "Bo you notice the clock above my head?

         Keep watching it, as it is approaching your deadline.    Also,

         there is one phone in the room. If anyone uses it, I will."       Leo

         and I resumed our conversation about trivia.    At five minutes to

         six, the German, General spoke, "You are Corso, aren't you?      We

         heard about you even before the surrender."    I replied, "Ask your

         friend who I am. I arrested him just after we took Rome."

               I got up from my chair and' said, "Your deadline has come and

         gone; we have nothing more to discuss."

           .   The General said, "Wait, please, we realized you would not

         fall for a bluff; but it was a silly but desperate gamble on my
            •
                                                     part.   The package is at this address.    (He handed me a slip o(


0000000000000000000000000000000000000000000
                                                     paper.)   There are two boys in the apartment; they are not armed.

                                                     The package is hanging out of the window."

                                                           I called Leo's Custom guards and gave them their

                                                     instructions.   They returned with the two boys and the package.

                                                     I looked at the two, turned to the Germans and said, "I am

                                                     surprised that men of your caliber would associate with such

                                                     scum.   I turned to Leo and instructed, "I don't even want them in


                                              i1     my prison.   Throw them out bodily, and give them a swift kick in

                                                     the rear as they exit."   Leo was built like a wrestler and

                                              iii    followed my instructions to a "T".

                                                           I opened the package; and after a cursory examination said,

                                                     "You two tried to bluff me and waste my time with these.      Why

                                                     didn't you give them to Angleton?      He would have made a, story

                                                     from them and called it an intelligence coup."

                                                           They sort of smiled and said, "What do you want from us?"

                                                     I said, "What were you doing with Angleton?"

                                                           They told me the entire story.    He had them rewriting the

                                                     Sunrise Operation," which was the surrender of the German armies

                                                     in Northern Italy, The intent was to give the credit for the

                                                     surrender to Allen Dulles and OSS in Switzerland.

                                                           They signed affidavits for me.    In return, I gave them a

                                                     letter saying they had cooperated with me.      I affixed my seal and

                                                     recommended that they should be released upon their return to

                                                    . Germany, through         channels.   My zeal assured safe passage

                                                     upon their departure.   The German General extended his hand to

                                                     me.   We shook hands, and I stepped back and saluted, which he

                                                     smartly returned.
                                                                      /Or


     Later, I threw copies of the affidavits on Angleton 's desk

with the warning, "Don't attempt any, and I mean any, operations

again in my area."     Checkmate!

     I had been overseas for close on to five years.      I believe
it took 35 points to go home.       I had amassed about 190.   The
points were given for campaigns, decorations, time overseas, etc.

In early 1947, Rome .Area Allied Command was getting ready to

dissolve.    General Dasher had gone; and in March 1947, I finaliy

received my orders to return to the United States.      After a round

of farewell parties by the Italian officials, I headed for

Livorno to board ship for the return to the States.

     Soon after my arrival, I received a phone call from

Angleton.    He said, "You must return for a few days and be seen

with me.    The word is out that I caused your transfer, and no one

will even talk to me."     I answered, "You sure tried very hard to

have me recalled, but in this case it was a normal transfer."

     He pleaded, "But the Italians don't know that.      I am going

to remain after the military is gone, and all intelligence which

our country needs is cut off."

     I told him, "It sounds strange, you spouting patriotism.

I'll be back tomorrow, but my day of embarkation is a week off;

and I will only stay in Rome for two days."

     Angleton and I were seen together in every bar and

restaurant in Rome.    This was the only time he ever acted

friendly towards me.    He brought up the Matter of my informants.

I answered, "Forget it."

     The ,low point in Angleton's life must have been the day in

Washington when I was appointed a Staff— member of the National
0 00 00000
                                              Security Council at the White !louse.   He made the remark to Frank

                                              Hand, a friend of mine, "The bad penny in back; I don't know how

                                       f•:j   he manages to get these high positions.;

                                                   I did get a copy of my C.I.A. file.   I was accused of

                                              kidnapping, murder, etc.   Angleton had put in that I used other

                                              people's intelligence and passed it off as my own.    The truth was
                                              that every month I compiled the official Rome Area Intelligence


 00000000000000000000000000000000000
                                              summary, with input from all agencies, and signed it AC of S G-2,

                                              which was normal, as it was my job to do so.   It must have

                                              bothered him tremendously when he realized he could not hide

                                              behind the "Top Secret" stamp or the cover, nI can't reveal my

                                              source."   I was above his position as head of counterintelligence

                                              of C.I.A., and could shove any fabrications or distortions down

                                              his throat!   Checkmate, again!

                                                   An interesting observation, which I must relate to, leads

                                              into the next episode.   I must state that many of my problems

                                              with SSU-CIG, which was under the Department of State, stem from

                                              ideological reasons.   During a meeting with high -level Italian

                                              officials, one of them said, "Corso, of course you realize that

                                              your Embassy is socialist."   It was an extension of the

                                              Department of State where they spawned American socialists.

                                                   I was not overly surprised by this Italian's assessment of

                                              the Embassy's staff, and I was later to extend the idea to much

                                              of the Department of State.   Thinking about this much more

                                              recently, it does appear that there is a kind of "mind set" held

                                              by most senior members of the State Department staff, a mind met

                                              which has become pretty much a'standard State approach toward

                                              socialism and communism generally, and the Soviet Union

                         MY GERMAN COUNTERPART

                           THE DEVIL KNIFE

     Duni-nrthis—peri,ed liki netted one of the most interesting men

I ever met -- Lieutenant Colonel Karl Haas, of the German Army.

The German Wermacht was mostly composed of honorable men and even

they had little use for the Gestapo and SS.       Karl Haas, for the

past two years, had been my counterpart in the German Army's

Military Intelligence.    We discussed the same operations, and how

he prepared them, how I caught them.       His version of the agents

that we played back with their radios was a success on our part.

He said it took them two weeks after the transmissions stopped to

realize we had supplied false information.        A five week

advantage, one of my most successful operations.

     Karl Haas, was a General Staff Officer at age 26 -- very

unusual, since the German General Staff was an elite group.          The

Italians used to ask me what the stars meant on my lapels.       I

used to answer, General Staff Corps.       They used to shake their

heads, "Yes, yes, now we understand why at age 27 you are in

charge of intelligence and security."       The more I tried to tell
                                              •


them it wasn't so in the United States Army, the more they smiled

and never really believed me.    If they found out later I was

appointed officially to the United States Army General Staff

Corps and wore the Green Eagle Badge, which, we called the "Green
00000000000 00000000000000000000000000000000
                                                    Hornet," on the left pocket of my blouse, then they would have

                                                    been sure they were right.

                                                         I used to take Haas to the Italian nightclubs, and there .

                                                    were always stares of disbelief among the Italians to see this

                                                    tall, blond German with me, tire Chief of United States Security.

                                                    We spoke mostly in Italian, although Haas spoke excellent

                                                    English.    I had his word of honor that he would not escape when

                                                    he was with me; however, he did escape the prison.

                                                         He had no place to go except to visit his old girl friends.

                                                    We caught him soon after. I sort of frightened him when I walked
                                               11   into the room and said, "I am going to shoot you."    After about

                                                    fifteen minutes, I °ate back in and said, "Haas, let's go eat."
                                               11
                                               2    He said, "I am sorry I escaped.   I knew you would get me, but I

                                                    had a good time until you caught me."

                                                         During one of our lighter moments, while having an after

                                                    dinner drink, Karl Haas said, "I have never told you that we once

                                                    held a meeting to discuss getting rid of you, because Rome had

                                                    been almost completely blacked out for intelligence, and the SS

                                                    and Gestapo were furious on how you ruthlesnly crushed their

                                                    agents.    They didn't like their methods being used against them.

                                                    The germanp high command was also angry over how you had caused

                                                    ,them considerable harm when you played back my agents.   I

                                                    objected to any attempt on your life.    But was over ruled."     Word

                                                    came back that you virtually butchered the first Gestapo hit man,

                                                    with a knife and shot him with his own gun.

                                                         I pulled my coat astde and said, "Karl, here is his gun and

                                                    here is the knife."   The devil knife appeared in my hand.    I

                                                    raised my glass to drink my after dinner drink, with the slight
                                                                     coc000c000c00000000000000000000000000000000
distraction it disappeared.   Karl, an unusual intelligent

individual said, "What a trick, no wonder we had trouble Sending

agents to "Get you,"   they were afraid of the devil knife, how do

you do it?   Would you, tell me?"   I answered, I see no harm in

telling you, the myth is now established.     And I think you can

attest to the fact that my perpetuation of it, even reached you

and had some effect on your operation."
0        THE STORY OF THE MAGIC KNIFE     •

0
0             Early in my tour in Rome, I decided to visit Switzerland

0        right after VE day.     The purpose of my visit was to go to 'Geneva

         and purchase a Path —Phillipe wristwatch.        This was a handmade

         watch and recognized as the world's most perfect timepiece. I

         bought a steel case Patek — Phillipe watch, since I didn't have

         enough money to buy a gold cased one.

              On my way back, I stopped in Milan, Italy, to visit friends

         at the local C,I.C.     On my way to the hotel, I stopped at a bar.

         I had an upset stomach, so the bartender offered me a "Campari"

         to settle my digestion.    I noticed a poster announcing a stage


    II   show musical.   The director's name was on the poster.,

         friend who had helped me ferret out communists in the Rome
                                                                      He was a


         theatrical community.    I looked at my new wristwatch and basked

         in the glow of perfection.    I guess the idea of its perfection

         made me feel good and was a constant reminder to emulate it.

              I saw that I had a half hour to curtain time.       T hailed a

         cab and made the show on time.       Durin,g intermission, I sent a

         note to the director.    The usher returned and asked me to

         accompany him backstage.    My friend was happy to see me, and I

         was invited to dine with the cast after the show.       I gladly

         'ccepted since they were delightful people.       One of the cast was

         a magician, and I had seen his act.      He made a knife appear in

         his hand and sliced an apple-, the knife disappeared, he began

         eating the apple, threw a handkerchief over it, shook it, and the

         apple came out whole again.
             I asked to meet the magicidn.    This was immediately

     arranged.     I was not interested in the apple, but I was intrigued

     by the appearing and disappearing knife.       When I was introduced,

     and told him I was interested in the knife disappearing trick, he

     was most cooperative.     At the pressure of his thumb, the blade of

     the knife would come out or retract into the handle.       The handle

     had two small pieces which snap fitted over a wire.       A spring

     wire with some small pulleys was attached to the sleeve of his

     coat.    The knife handle snapped onto the wire.     A slight pressure

     would ease the knife into his hand.       Thumb pressure released the

     spring loaded blade or retracted it.       I paid him well for one of
2,   the sleeve systems.    (His arm had to be bare because he often,
 1
3    during his act, pulled up his sleeve.)       Upon my return to Rome, I

     had a duplicate of my stilleto made with a retracting blade.         And

     so was born the illusion of the devil knife which strangely could
 1   appear or disappear in my hand.    I brought the contraption home,

     and instead of a knife I attached a derringer to it. I often

     thought, what a phony act for a United States Army Officer to

Li   play.    But, on the other hand, any trick, clean or dirty, was

     justified in order to protect the lives of the Americans in Rome.

     My C.I.C. agents were constantly on mit to show them how I did the

     trick.    I used to.laugh and say "Magic."     Especially when the

     fake knife would disappear, I would pull up my sleeve and reach

     Back and pull out the real knife.       This was always good for

     another round of drinks.    As I ended my story, I reached, back and

     drew the real knife and said, "Karl,. this is the real knife not

     an illusion."    He said, "May I see it?"     I handed it to him.    He

     turned it over and over and said, "Beautiful."

                                                     He leaned back and said, "Yes, I understand and we both will

                                                survive.    I. am glad I met you."   "Yes, Karl, I suppose people

                                                Like us are survivors."

                                                     That night I made him an offer.      I said, "Many criminals are

                                                escaping to South America.      During this period of turmoil, why

                                                don't you go to South America and help us keep track of these

                                                criminals."    He accepted..   We sent him to the C.I.C. in Germany.

                                                I never heard from him again.

                                                     I have often thought about Karl Haas and wondered what might

                                                have happened to him.    At the time recently of the Klaus Barbie

                                                .business in France, I've wondered whether our people in Germany

                                                saw the potential in him that seemed self evident to me.

                                                Certainly, there would be no moral issue in using him for our

                                                side in the never ending "Cold War" against the communists.         Or,

                                                         UNMASKING THE "DEVIL" KNIFE


                                                              During a conference with General Dasher, I brought up the

                                                         subject that, "The Italians had wined and dined us many times and

                                                         I would like ,to reciprocate.   I would like to invite the Chief of

                                                         Police of Italy, the Chief of Italian Intelligence and their

                                                         respective staffs.   Interspersed with my C.I.C. and F.S.C. agents

                                                         who spoke Italian.   I would try to save enough from the

                                                        .confidential funds to defray. expenses."     General Dasher answered,

                                                         "I think you have a good idea Phil, and I will invite, General

                                                         Smith, from AFFIG, and ask General Lowe.'    I will make funds
                                                   Li
                                                         available from our headquarters money.      Go ahead with the planth."

                                                              My old friend, Mario, the headwaiter at the G.I. restaurant,
                                              2          had now become Major - demo at a beautiful nightclub, north of

                                              it         Rome, on the ocean, at a place called Fregene.      It would make an
                                              2                                                                    I sat down
                                                         ideal setting. I called Mario and said I was coming.

                                                         with him and the owner'.   I began, "I will be the host for

                                                         cocktails and a dinner for the Chief of Police of Italy, the
                                              fi
                                                        ..Chief of         their staffs, my commanding General and other

                                                         high ranking officers, and gentlemen, I want to Make a "Buon

                                                         Figura" (good showing). To an Italian restaurant owner this is

                                                         virtually a challenge similar as a."Duel of Death."      They both

                                                        .stood up and said, "Senor, go back. to Rome and leave everything

                                                         to us.   You .have our word and we stake our honor, this will be an

                                                         occasion long to be remembered.    We will do yqu honor."     I shook

                                                         hands with both and said, "I know friends, this is why I came

     The night of the dinner I arrived early, mainly to check

security arrangements.    A private area was cordoned off and it

was beautifully decorated with flowers and shrubs and moveable

walls with 'murals done in excellent taste.    There was one waiter
for every two guests.    I lined them up and each received a carton

of cigarettes and 20,000 lire.    When the guest began to arrive.

The waiters brought out a huge swan, carved. from ice.   The center
between the wings was hollow and filled with a special cocktail

mixture created for the occasion.     We had the usual delicious

appetizers.    I always had to be careful not to make a full meal

of them.   The 'main course was turkey. Beautiful large birds of

over 20 1b8.    They had drumsticks, breast, wings, etc., but the

surprise was they were boneless and molded to look real and all

white meat with all the trimmings.    There were two desserts.     One

a huge mound of cream and inside was chopped cooked chestnuts

mixed with chocolate. The other was a half, round watermelon with

a handle of the rind.    Gold strands of brown sugar covered it.

Inside was chopped melon mixed with chocolate and cream.    The

entire meal and service was outstanding and a work of art and a

model of efficiency.

     I interpreted for General Dasher ana the Chief of Police,           Co
Ferrari.   Colonel Morena, Chief of C.I.C. translated for General

Smith of AFHQ and General Depasquale, Chief of S.I.M. Major

Downs, Head of my British FSC, interpreted for Brigadier Lowe and

Colonel Middleton, Chief of Staff of Rome Area Allied Command.

     After dinner General Dasher came to me and said, "I see no

security, but knowing you, it mbst be as tight as a drum.     I

said, "Yes, Sir, I took no chances.    The road from Fregene to
                                                                         •


Rome has a celere jeep, a C.I.C. jeep or FSC cars every quarter

mile.       Prefect Moro is a guest, but he set up the road 'patrols.

My customs guards guard the perimeter of the restaurant.          The
doorman and attendants are Italian Police.       The waiters are my

paid waiters from the Club 57, in Rome.       Note, each is armed.

Your waiter and the Chief of Policeg, is Lieutenant Franco of the

Customs Guards who I consider the best.       The two headwaiters

Mario and Paolo are veterans and exceptional individuals."          He

smiled and at that moment Chief Ferrari came up and I should not

have been surprised when the conversation turned to the "Devil

Knife" and Chief Ferrari put his arm around me.

       The next morning I was called by General Dasher.     He spoke

of the dinner in glowing terms.       He said, "It solidified our

relationship and was well worth the expense."       The tab was

$1,8000.00 and $200.00 from my confidential funds to feed the

security guards and I made sure my favorites the "Celere" squad

were well taken care of for the highway security.
   111N15
    (The General-Drasker hit me with a small bomb which put me in

a temporally uncomfortable position when he said, "Phil, what is

this "Devil's Knife" which you carry and Chief Ferrari speaks of

in such glowing terms.      He said it strikes terrbr in the hearts

of our enemies. 'If there is such a thing I would like to see

it."

       I stuttered and mumbled, "Yes, Sir"     I reached back and

pulled out the stiletto.      The General had a slight smile and a

look of surprise, He said, "Let me see it."       I handed it to him

and said, be .careful Sir, it is razor sharp."      I blushed at his

next remark., "But Chief Ferrari said it will only out at its,
                                                                              ' PA'


    masters' command."     He pressed the buzzer and said, "Edwin, come

    in here I want to show you something."      I had a sinking feeling

    in my stomach, now the old intelligence pro, Brigadier General

    Lowe would evaluate the situation,    General Dasher snid, "Look at

    this Edwin."     He held up the stilleto.   General Dasher said, "It

    belongs to our G-2 and Chief Ferrari said it strikes terror in

    the hearts of our enemies and is known as the "Devil's K nife."       I

i   tried to explain, "I perpetuate the myth because it is in our

    best interest."

           Brigadier Lowe, then surprisingly said, "My chaps have

    mentioned that it appears and disappears like magic.     Is that

    so?"   I said, meekly, "yes, Sir."   I straightened my arm and the

    magician knife appeared in my hand.    General Dasher exclaimed,

    "I'll be damned!"    It disappeared, and he added, "Edwin', we have

    a magician in our midst." I explained, "It is done by simple

    pulleys and a spring. A magician taught me,"     They both laughed

    and Brigadier Lowe, the old fox said, "In our trade everything

    goes."   He turned to me and continued, "Good show, Charles this

    boy will go far and surviye."    General Dasher answered, "I am

    sure he will."    I left, I had been sweating, and I felt better

    now that these two wonderful men knew, understood, and approved

    my un-officer like act.

                                ECONOMIC INTELLIGENCE

                                   "THE PIRATE"


            During the end of 1945 and in 1986, I saw the situation

       changing.   Mopping-up operations were over.' We turned our

       internment camps over to the Italians, and all trials of security

       threats and collaborators were given to the Italian judicial

if?    system.   Our constitutional lawyers, ACLU, and human rights

       activists will squirm and beat their chest at the system which
dr     was established.   In cases of dangerous individuals, such as

I]     saboteurs or agent provocateurs, the sentence Was always set

       before the case went to trail.
11          At this juncture, an event happened which adjusted my flow

       of information to the changing mission -- economic intelligence.

       nardhese Serena, the shipping magnate who kwd introduced me to

       the Borgia Princess, who wanted to toudh my, knife, called me.        He

       was terribly agitated.    He said, "Filippo, there are

       demonstrations, and the police have cordoned off the area.       My

       brother and niece' are in the area.    He has a temper, and I am

       afraid he might do something rash and get hurt; can you help me?"

       T told him, "I will pick .you up.in ten minutes."     I took a jeep

       and 'picked him up at his office.     Marchese Serena was known in

       ItalThn circles as "The Pirate."    When I came into Rome, the

       allied list of people to arrest had his name on it.      He was a

       suspected collaborator, because he ran his ships which carried
                                                                                   70
a
         food -- fruit, fish and vegetables -- from the islands to Italian

         cities.     I found out the collaboration charge was unfounded.      He
         was a man of considerable courage and defied the Nazis.      This


    IT   fact was confirmed by the Vatican authorities.       He and his

         family, with most of hia wealth, stayed in the Vatican when we

         were advancing and the Gestapo was looking for him.      He was

         Jewish but married to a Catholic.

                 When I released him, he came to my office.   He said, "You

         did your duty, and I thank you for clearing my name of suspicion.

         But, above all, I wanted to meet the man who strikes terror into

         the hearts of th,ose inhuman types in the prison; and they speak

         of you in hushed tones."    I remember answering him with a laugh,

         "You see, Marchese, I am not a monster; in fact, I don't even

         look the part."

a               When we reached the police cordon, I stopped and said,
a   r i "Marchese, wait here with the police; I will be back in about a
a
a   si
         half hour."    He angrily answered, "Absolutely not; you are taking

         a risk for my family, at my request -- we go together!"      I saw I

a        could not dissuade this type of individual.    So I said, "All -
a        right, but you do as I say."    He answered, "Yes, naturally."       I

         looked ,at him, "Give me your pistol; it is useless against a

         mob."    I handed it .to the police officer along with my pistol and

         told him to hold them until my return.    Then I inStructed •Serena,

a        "if we are stopped by communist provocateurs, they may recognize

         you.    You are my prisoner and, if necessary,. I will do the

         talking."    Then I instructed the police officer, "Call Prefect

         More, and tell.hiM I am going to this address (which I repeated)

         and will be back in a half hour.    He saluted and said, "Si, Senor
      'Colonello.'"

              When we reached our destination, there was a crowd in an

      ugly mood yelling, "Hail to the Republic."      This was before the.

      election. I drove through the crowd and stopped in a clear spot

      from the building.     I told the Marchese, "Wait here."     I walked
      through the crowd.     I was wearing a short Eisenhower jacket.       It
      pulled up in the back, and my stilleto was visible.       I heard
      exclamations, "E lui."    "It is him."   The occupants had been

      watching, and the Marchese 'a brother and his young daughter came

      out.     I greeted them and said, "Veni."   "Come."   I put them in

      the jeep., and we, drove off.   Fearless outer appearance, perhaps;

      but my shirt under the jacket was soaking wet from perspiration,

      and it was a cool day.

              When we reached the police checkpoint, there was Prefect

      Moro, with his entire ""Celere Squad," lined up in riot — ready

      formation.     He saluted, handed me our pistols and said, "You

      could have waited; I would have cleared the area."        I said, "But,

      my friend, there was no danger."     He looked at me and said, "Sc

      you always say."    I answered, "Thank you, Moro, and thank your

Cu:   men; I will see you at the stable tomorrow.     Have 'Corso' ready,

      and we will go riding."

              As we rode between the "Celerc" jeeps, the men all snapped

      at attention.    I heard the young girl ask her Father, "Who is

      he !'

              For years Serena's sailors had been gathering intelligence

      for me from every corner of the Mediterranean.        When I went

      aboard their ships, which were island hoppers, I was always

      greeted like a visiting dignitary and served a delicious meal.
                                                                                                                                  I DV


                                                         Soon after the episode, where I picked up the Marchests brother

                                                         and niece, I was invited to dinner at his home.

                                                                                                             When I arrived,

                                                         there was present a group of distinguished — looking men.        They.
                                                         all arose when I entered.     I was introduced to each by Serena.

                                                         Represented were the largest industrialists in Italy:     for

                                                         example, Salvatore Scslera, the man who had built most of the

                                                         railroads, harbors and roads in Italy.     I sensed that the wily

                                                         old Pirate Serena had something up his sleeve.     I was sure it
                                                   -F1
                                                         would develop in a hurry.     The sumptuous dinner was served and

                                                         ended with small talk conc• erning the political situation in
                                               n
                                               if Italy.            What was .unusual of the gathering was, that there were no

                                               -         wives present.     After dinner, we retired to the large living room

                                                         for after dinner drinks.     Marchese Serena finally began,

                                                 LI "Gentlemen, we are proud to have here as our honored guest, the
                                               31:
                                                    Head of Security and Intelligence of the Rome area Allied .

                                                         Command.    He will tell you he doesn't look the part; but I will
                                               a:
                                               1] tell yo-u, friends, he fills the part.           Don't be fooled by his

                                                         boyish, soft—spoken, easy going approach.     Our enemies will
                                                     1
                                               -


                                                         attest to that.    The enemies of Italy respect and fear him with

                                                         good cause.    I will not have to cover his exploits.   You know

                                                         them.   I have seen him act with my own eyes.    The Church, our

                                                         government, our army, our security forces respect him above all

                                                         others who have come to our shores.     We owe him a great debt of

                               CHAPTER XIII


                             ITALIAN COMMUNISM

                                    vs.

                             RUSSIAN COMMUNISM


         In Rome I rubbed shoulders with the nobility, partied with

 the jet —set, dined with the social elite, conferred with the

 heads of the government, met with the Captains of industry,

 honored by the Church, and had the respect and admiration of the

 Italian military and security forces; but much of my intelligence

'came from my association and identity with the grass roots and

 the underground.

         The United States Army was subsidizing a group of civilian

 restaurants in Rome.     A soldier on rest and recuperation could

 get a steak dinner with all the trimmings for about 50 cents.

 One G.I. I knew, ate five eggs and two steaks for breakfast for

 one dollar.    Periodically, I visited these restaurants, generally

 accompanied by an Italian police official or my giant carabiniere

 aide, Visconti, in uniform, filled with all of his 6' 7" frame                C)

 and weighing 300, pounds.    I had two purposes, check the food and           C)

 make sure security was adequate.
                                                                               CD
      The soldiers were usually on leave from combat units and                 C)
'tended at times to be rowdy.     One day I walked in, and there was

 a group of about a dozen soldiers at a table.     They were drinking

 wine.    One of them saw me and said, "Hey, Colonel, you slumming?"

I disregarded the crack.      I saw the Italian waiter talking to
00000000000
                                     Li   him.    At that point, Visconti walked in.    The same soldier

                                          resented the reference to the loyal Visconti and bristled.         I

                                          started toward the table; there was a hush.        I was wearing my

                                          summer uniform, and the blouse had a slit on the lower right.           I

                                          said, "I wasn't a british commando, but I do carry a knife."           In

                                          a quick snap I stuck, with a thud, the stiletto on the table.           It

         There were some heated arguments on communism, and I could cuss

         and swear 1n Italian with the best of them.     Usually, we sat at a
         large table.   Sometimes as many as twenty were seated.    One night
         two of them got in a name - calling match and were ready to come

         to blows.   One reached for a knife.    I stepped in and said, "When

        I am eating and drinking with my friends, I. don't like

        disruptions such as this; it isn't good for my digestion.;
:711    Then in Sicilian dialect I said, "Ti tago        coda con il cotedo."

        This meant "I will out your throat with a knife."      The headwaiter

        and some of the others were Sicilian, and they understood.        The

        others didn' .   The headwaiter translated and said, " 'He will

        cut your throat with his knife.'    Sit down because you know he

:11     might do it."    The rumpus quieted down, and I ordered wine for

        everyone.

               The headwaiter turned to me and said, "You never cease to

        amaze me.    You speak the Sicilian dialect; where did you learn

        it?"    I replied, "My parents came to America in 1902, and my

        Grandfather spoke only in dialect.      I was quick to learn and can


  1     speak it very well."

               Prefect Moro, of the Lightning Squad was a Sicilian.       When

        we cdnversed in dialect, his men were amused; since the ordinary

        Italian cannot understand the dialect.      My liaison officer,

        Captain Mario Bolaffio, a Lawyer by trade, could not understand

       '.us when I spoke to his boss, General Pompeo Agrifoglio, Chief of

        Italian Intelligence, in dialect.    To better Understand my

        mastery of the dialect, I went to the hometown, near Palermo,

        where my parents were born.    A relative of mine took me one night

        to a small dinner with the town elders.      When we left, I asked
cl00000000000000000ci000000000000000000000000
                                                him, "It seemed that they wanted to keep me talking; Why?"      He

                                                said, "You fascinated them and sent chills up their spines; it

                                                     My Grandparents had left forty years before, my photographic

                                                Italy will never be a Soviet satellite.    We take their money and

                                                   :Subsepient events, years later, proved him correct.       I must
have done something right when men such as this, trusted me with

           From such relative unplanned trivia are close relationships

Ti         They loved to hear stories of when I    Was   growing up in the

      small coal mining town, California, Pennsylvania.        They even

      found it on a map, in the hilly country of southwest

      Pennsylvania.    I was a good story teller; and they loved the

      descriptions of our schools, parties, dances, college, football,

      baseball, girlfriends, my old 1929 Ford, etc.

           They especially loved the stories of my family.       One story,

      in particular, they often wanted me to repeat to their friends.

      I must have told it a dozen times.    It concerned my blackberry

      keg of wine.    It began:

           My Father had a week off from the steel mill, so we decided

      to make blackberry wine.     I was 18 years old. We went out every

      day, and when we returned home, we would dump the day's picking
 S.


      in a 55 gallon wine keg.     We filled it in one week.    I went out

      and bought a 10 gallon Coca Cola syrup keg, washed it out,           and

                                   mashed enough blackberries to fill the keg.       I added 12 kilos of

                                   sugar, since I wanted it sweet.        My Father made a 55 gallon keg,

                                   mine had sugar added, naturally it fermented quicker.          Around
                                   October, it stopped fermenting; and I plugged it.          My Father's

                                   wine was still fermenting.     One day my Father said, "Phil, let's

                                   keg with a spigot bung, and the wine was excellent.          Needless to

                                   say, my rather invited so many of his friends that between

                                   drinking and gifts to take home, my 10 gallons of sweet wine

                                   quickly disappeared.   I didn't really care, because I liked to

                                   sit in with them and listen to their conversations in Sicilian

                                   dialect.   But, even, years later, I loved to rub it in to mV

                                   Father on how he tricked me out of my special wine.

                                         The Italians loved this story; perhaps because it was so

                                   down to earth, and they could closely relate with the tale and

                                   live the part of the characters in the story.       Another plus on my

                                   side, with each story I became more a part of their lives.

                                         I told them stories of my wife, Eunice, How I met her, basic

                                   training and how I became an officer.       I'told a little story of
      my wife and the deep south.       They had an intense interest in my

      down to earth stories of the deep south. Terms such a "Damn
                                        • . • __-
      Yankee" "Dago", "White inw", 'EW-1Weicitr..6-en-e,"(et.p. II had to try to

      explain certain things to them for hours on end. Although I

      explanations became almost legendary.  It seemed like the

      following story, which had no ending becameilly most famous tale.

           At completion of basic training, April 1942, I was kept as a

      member of the cadre.     Soon after, forty of us and three officers,

      were chosen tn establish a new Army post near Ft. McLellan,

      Alabama.   ,We were told that we were chosen for our versatility

      and as soldiers who could perform With little or no direction.

      One Saturday, a friend of mine and I, had a two day pass, so we

      decided to go to Birmingham, Alabama.        On the way to the highway,

      where we were going to hitch hike, we stopped in a small park,

      where I saw two girls sitting on a bench, one was the most

      beautiful golden haired girl I had ever seen. Birmingham was                 a
      forgotten.   We both made our way to the bench.         The red head was

      to become my future wife Eunice-. The other was her sister, Jo.

           Later, I -found out.that I began with three strikes against

      Tie with her family.    They found out I was Italian, Catholic, and

        Dam' Yankee. to boot.    Eunice's Brother - in - Law was a Baptist

      minister, and her Father had been a soldier in the United States

      Army, in the Philippines where - they were chasing A'Guinaldo, at

      the beginning of the Century.       He had told the girl's that. he did

      not want them to date soldiers.       Later, the Reverend Green, the

      Baptist minister, took a liking to me.        I was the only Catholic

      0.e had ever met. I will never forget the look of pride on her
0000000
                                       Father's face when almost ten-years later, I went to visit him in

                                       the Veteran's hospital in Montgomery, Alabama.            He made it a

                                       point to introduce his cronies to his Son - in - Law, the

                                       Colonel, and made sure they noticed my decorations.

                                            Eunice told me that her Brothers used to say, "Dad, get the

                                       shotgun; here comes that "damn yankee - Dago."            I never knew

                                       whether my dark complexion and deep tan were elements in their

                                       thinking, but Eunice prevailed, and became my wife.

                                            I was made Sergeant, and while she was sewing on my stripes,

                                       I announced that I had been accepted at officer's school and :

                                       would be leaving in a week.       I pulled out a little box.       It

                                       contained a diamond engagement ring on which I had spent my lust

                                       4140.00.   She accepted.    I returned almost four months later:. as a

                                       brand new Second Lieutenant.

                                       When we first came south, we were briefed that there were certain

                                       customs in which we would have no part, and so to act

                                    CHAPTER XIV


                                       ROOTS


            The Italians from the Prime Minister, the Chief of Police of

      Italy, through my customs guards, down the rank and file of the

      Police, to my working class, card playing friends all used the

      term "You are one of ours."    The Italian military said I was the

      only foreigner to whom they gave the "War Cross" for military

       valor, which was reserved for their soldier!.   I asked why?   The

      answer was always, "You are one of ours, you have been here

       before."   I never questioned this mystical thinking, because it

      was in my best interest, the United States Army's interest and

      that of the United States soldiers interest whose well being was

      my responsibility, and my mission which was in the interest of

      Italy.

:1         The Italians were for the most part a deeply religious

      people and as such, believed in, an hereafter.    Reincarnation was

      also one of their beliefs.    They also knew that I was only one

      generation removed from an Italian birth.    My Mother and Father

      were born in Italy and I spoke, fluently, the dialect of my

      Grandparents.    Although, I had never thought much about

      reincarnation some of the events which happendd to me, often made

      .me wonder.   I often tried to reason, is this so-called

      reincarnation really a. thought - mind process more than a

      physical one, was my mind the type, that like a radio receiver,
3.]
      could tune in and draw waves from another time.    However, I was
                                                                                                                             3-1-"


0000003000000000000000000000000000000000 000
                                                 an kntelligence officer and had to deal in facts, not wild

                                                fantasies.   As I look back I    Was   like a business man whose every

                                                venture turns into a profit and he becomes a millionaire.         In

                                                Rome, my operation was identical.       It seemed like every action I

                                                took or planned succeeded.      Was I tufned in to a computer that

                                                was all ready programed and was I part of this programming which

                                                came from the past into the present then into the future.

                                                Whatever it was I am grateful, because the protective screen I

                                                established took care of our soldiers.       I will leave the mystic

                                                Part for others to understand and give a final answer'.      This is

                                                not mine to reason why.

                                                     Now I want to come down from the occult to reality and my

                                                roots.   Early    in my tour, I was called by the General.      He had

                                                received instructions that we would escort diplomats from enemy

                                                countries, Germany, Japan, Rumania, etc. and now in the Vatican,

                                                to the resort at Taormina, Sicily, for internment, until they

                                                could be shipped to their home countries.       The Vatican was

                                                sending Monsignor McGeough, my old friend from the Vatican's

                                                American College.    Because of the sensitive nature of the mission

                                                and since the war was still raging, General Dasher gave me the

                                                responsibility for the security of the group.       There were about     -

                                                forty people, men, woman and children.       I was ordered to

                                                personally accompany the group and take as many agents as needed.

                                                We arrived at Taormina.    It was one of the most beautiful resort

                                               'areas I have ever seen.    I told my men, "I wouldn't mind being

                                                interned here."    We had a good time at-the bar. I was billeted

                                                with Monsignor McGeough.    When I went to my room, I flipped on

                                                the light.   The Monsignor was praying on his knees.      I put out
                                                                            0
     the light and slinked back to the bar.      When I went back he was

     asleep.     Early the next morning I was awakened my the Monsignor.

     I had a hangover, I said, "Go away and let me sleep."       He said
                                                                            0
     softly, "Get up, I am going to say mass and you will be the only       0
     one attending."    I put my head under the pillow and said, "I am      0
F'
     sick and you are a Monsignor and you should pity a dying man."
                                                                            0
     He had no pity and I had to accompany him.      After his mass we

     went to breakfast, and he said, "Don't you feel better now that        0
     you went to mass"    I muttered, "No."    He laughed.   I muttered,    0
     you Monsignors are all the same they must train people like you
                                                                            0
     and O'Flaherty in the same school."      He smiled and said, "You      0
     said it, we lookout for people with hangovers."

          The escort left to return to Rome and British Field Security
                                                                            0
                                                                            0
     (FSG) took over security of the group.      T did not go back with     0
     the others. Instead I caught a military flight to Palermo.       I     0
     decided to go visit the small town where my parents and

     grandparents were born. In Palermo I teamed up with some British

     Officers.    They were going to Catania, driving along the northern

     coast of Italy.    They offered to drive me to Campo Felice, a

     small town about 20 miles east of Palermo.     My British friends
                                                                            0
     asked me if I knew the place.    I said, "No, but I am sure I will
                                                                            0
     recognize it, because I have heard my parents and grandparents         0
     describe it a thousand times.     About 20 miles out of Palermo, I

     asked the driver to stop.     A winding walled road wound up the

     hill for about a half mile.    I could see the town on top.    To my

     right I could see the plain which sloped to the sea.      One of the

     officers said, "We will drive you up to the village."       I said,

     "No, a thousand times I have walked up this road with my parents.
                                                                         )


 I prefer to go on foot."    He responded, "We understand, Good

 luck."

       As I approached the foot of the wall, a small boy of about

 10 years of age was sitting there.       He called out, "Americana,

 who are you?"    I'answered, "Corso."     He took off running up the

 road.    By the time I reached the piazza about 50 people were

 waiting.    I never knew I had so many relatives.    (Later, I asked

 my Dad and he said most were not related.)       My Father's oldest

 brother lived in the village.    He was out in the orchard.      The

 small boy who announced my arrival took me out.      They were

 picking figs.    When they saw me approach     I was in uniform.   As

 soon as I was introduced, my Uncle embraced me and they tried to

stuff me with figs.     I stayed with, my Uncle, but it appeared that

 the entire village came to meet me in his home.      They were

 especially fascinated that I conversed with them in dialect,

 which in most cases was all they knew.

       The next morning, just after breakfast, a villager came in

 and said, "They are here again."       I asked my Uncle, "Who is

 here?"   He said, "Two British soldiers and an Italian have been

 coming to' small villages, such as ours, looking for allied

materials.    They loot whatever they want and we can do nothing."

I went to my rpom took my imb belt and buckled it on, and I told

my Uncle, "Show me where they are."

       As we rounded the corner, I saw an Italian leaning against a

car.     When he saw me he stiffened.    I drew my stiletto half way

.out of its scabbard.   I said, in dialect, "If you move you will

be picking this knife out of your throat.       Put your hands on your

head and keep them there."    Two soldiers came out of the house

                                                waved back and cheered.     Itguess I left as some sort of hero.        I

                                                     One small episode happened on the train.      The mail car

                                                happened.   They began to argue.    Soon it was bedlam.   I put up

                                                hollered,, "Silence! If I hear another word before we get to

                                                Palermo, I will stop Ule train and have this car cleared."         It

                                                was torture to a Sicilian to make him keep quiet.      They looked

                                                daggers at me, but there was no more noise until we arrived: in

                                                Palermo.

                                                     My plane for Rome was leaving the next morning.      In the

                                                afternoon I went into a small food store.     I told the clerk in my

                                                best dialect that I wanted a small package of salami, cheese and

                                                olive oil to take back to Rome.    He kept looking at me and took

                                                off for the back room.     The two owners came out and excitedly,

                                                the clerk was explaining that the American officer was talking in

                                                dialect, just like them.    All activity ceased.   They began to

                                                slice bread and cheese, brought out wine and even the customers

                                                joined in to welcome the Sicilian — American back home.     When I

                                                told them about my visit to Campo Felice, the clerk who had

                                                waited on me said, with pride,."I told you he was one of ours."

                                                    This was the first time I heard the phrase.      An ordinary

                                                clerk first used the phrase that later I heard from the Prime
iii
                         Minister of Italy.                     As I was leaving on a plane the next morning,

                         I looked down to the land of my roots and later realized it
                         contributed to my success in Rome.
