                                                              Dos!                 Commendatore - my brilliant aide.    You
                                                                                   will go back to Interpol in a top
                                                                                   position, which you deserve.

                                                              Mario Bolaffio   -   Captain in the Italian Army, i Lawyer
                                                                                   and my liaison officer. I said, 'Thank
                                               ;•5
                                                                                   you Major, you smoothed over many rough
                                                                                   spots and taught me how to understand
                                                                                   much of which I knew little or nothing.
                                                                                   You have been with me three years, and I
                                                                                   think you.'

                                                              Pear the end of dinner, Commedatore Dos!, the Interpol

                                                         Executive, unrolled a scroll.   He had written a song about me,

                                                         which, much to my i-egret, I lost the scroll during the move.   In

                                                         later years, the nuns in Karlsruhe, Germany, wrote me i song

                                                         which the children of the orphanage sang to me at Christmas.

                                               ill       Both songs were carelessly lost in the moves.

                                                              After forty years, I still look back; and a feeling of pride

                                                         comes over me that such men had accepted me as their leader and

                                                         commander.

                                                                    CHAPTER XVII


                                                                     CONCLUSION

                                                                  THE WINNING SCORE

                                                                        0-0-0


                                            0 - American soldiers killed; 0 - installations damaged; 0 -

                                       men and agents killed or wounded.    I was, and I still am, proud

                                       of this record while I was Chief of Security in Rome.       I like to

                                       repeat these statistics.    I often, reflect, how did I do it, and

                                  TI


   0000000000000000000000000000
                                       what were the contributing factors?      In my opinion, it was my

                                       relationship lath ordinary people, such as the common policeman,

                                       the rank - and - file carabiniere, and Customs guards, the lower

                                       - level government employees, and the working class.       During the

                                       day, I was the proper United States Army Staff Officer.       I had my

                                       green uniforms made from a sharkskin cloth; my summer uniforms

                                       were not khaki, but gabardine,.all tailor made and fitted.       These

                                       were my uniforms for official' appearances.      My civilian clothes,'

                                       at 1944 prices, were raw silk suits ($25.00 each) and tailor -

                                       made suits ($75.00) for the social functions and dinners.       For my

                                       night wanderings, I wore a "T" shirt, slacks, and often sandals,

                                       and a sport jacket when it was cool.       For official functions, I

                                       rode in the chauffeUred former German Ambassador's Mercedes -

                                       Bentz.   During the day, to and from work,.I used a Jeep.      For my

                                       night. excursions) I drove a "Topolino" (small mouse), a Fiat that

                                       .looked like a Volkswagen Bug.

                                            Quite 'often, after the card games, I would drive some of the
                                        •


                                                         . '
                                                                     4170
                                                                        C)
                                                                        C)
     players home. They loved my "Topolino" with its souped - up
                                                                        C)
     engine. I always asked to come up for a cafe." At three or four    C)
     in the morning, they would wake up the wife, and the kids would    CD
     follow. (Prefect Moro's children called me "Zio"
                                                                        CD
                                                           (Uncle.)        The
                                                                                 C)
     women would always beraten their husbands for bringing, as they             C)
     said, "a distinguished guest" at this ungodly hour; and they were           ()
                                                                                 C)
     ashamed that I should see them just out of bed.       However, they
                                                                                 C)
     were always proud that I would visit with them at any time.
                                                                                 C)
          These grass - roots contacts grew and spread.       Information        C)

     dame to me day and night.   Many a drunken G.I. was cared for
                                                                                 ()
     until they could get me or the police.       If anyone asked questions
                                                                                 ()
     concerning me or my whereabouts, I was instantly told,       I              C)
     estimate that about 50 percent of my information came from these            C)
                                                                                 C)
     supposedly low - level sources.

          But, all this leads up to the real reason for any success              C)
     that I had.   From the lowest levels of society to the top, I was            C)

     able to speak their language..    My ability to speak dialects was
                                                                                  C)
     always a pleasant surprise.   If I had to use an interpreter, my
                                                                                 ()
     effectiveness would have been reduced 50 to 60 percent.          No         (.

     language barrier was the real reason I succeeded.      This made it
                                                                                  (.
     possible to fit in and blend into all levels and opened up

     invaluable sources of information.       The end result was 0-0-0, no

     American soldiers or security agents killed or injured and no

1   .Unites States installations damaged.       This was one game where a &Zino c

    .score won, and the by - product was a free Italy.

                                      EPILOGUE:    RETURN TO ROME - 1959 - FIFTEEN YEARS LATER


                                      In 1959, I was Commander of the list Missile Battalion at

                                 Karlsruhe, in West Germany.    I had just received my orders to

                                 become Inspector General of the 7th United States Army, also

                                 stationed in West Germany.    My friend, Ezio Rapanelli, had

                                 written to me that I was a legend to his Sons, and they and his

                                 Father wanted to meet me.    I wrote back that if he would come to

                                 visit me at my Battalion Headquarters before I lett my command, I

                                 would come to Italy.   If he came here, I would make him feel like

                                 a General, and parade a crack United States Army Combat Battalion

                                 in his honor.    He came with his wife and hiS sons.   I took him' on

                                 a tour of my installations and told him, "They are the best and

                                 the proudest."    He was in form and answered, "Don't brag to me. I

                                 expected nothing less of soldiers under your command."


      000000000000000000000000
                                      Shortly after, we went to Italy.    His home was in Foligno,

                                 Italy, about 60 miles north of Rome, in the providence     or
                                 Perugia.   I drove through Munich, south to Austria, and entered

                                 Italy at the Brenner Pass. .I carried no passport, only military

                                 orders and my Identification card.   These I had to show to the

                                 Italian border guards, and I told them my destination was

                                 Foligno, Italy.   The news must have traveled quickly, because I

                                 received a message at Foligno. that the Commanding General of the

                                 Garabiniere, Italy's elite police force, requested my presence

                                 and my wife's at a dinner in my honor, in Rome.

                                     Just before this first visit to Rome in 1959         returned

                                 again in 1974 -- another 15 years later),   I had some
       apprehension:   Did I really want, to go back?    What would I find?
       Who   would I see?    How would Rome appear?   Did I set a foundation
ii     in 1944-47?   Were Rome/Italy visibly or otherwise better off for

       what I had done?     Many of my friends and acquaintances, the Chief

       of Italian Intelligence, the Chief of Police of Italy, the Prime

       Minister Aloide De Gaspar', Pope Pius XII, Monsignor Montini

      (Pope Paul VI), Monsignor O'Flaherty, and others had passed on.

      Would they, in hindsight, look down and say, "It would have

       happened regardless; or, you did a fine job."
er,
a                 When I arrived at the dinner, the Commanding General of

      .the Carabiniere greeted me with the members of his staff and the

      former Commander and shaft of the Rome Garrison.       Also present

      were my former colleague and friend, General De Pasquale, former

      Commanding General of Italian Intelligence; and the first

      Intelligence Chief of NATO.      Many of my 'former friends on his

      staff were present.     There were no handshakes; each in turn

      embraced me, and each in turn bowed and kissed Euniceis,hand.            I

      felt good and most happy at tile greetings.     This was a greeting

      of old friends with a sign 'of great respect and mutual admiration

      that had, been nourished in a time of stress and danger.        These

      were not wealthy men, but tHey had traveled at their own expense

      and paid for the dinner from their pockets to honor a friend.

             During the speeches, some of my questions were answered.

      ;The Commander of the Carabiniere announced that, but for me,           he

      and many others present would not be here today.      General

      De Pasquale told how I had assisted and helped 'reorganize a

      shattered intelligence service.     But the most important item that

      eame,omt of the. speeches was that during the period, both France
              and Italy were in danger of going communist and becoming Soviet

              satellites; and how I understood and performed a dual function.

              On one hand, I rebuilt the Italian security forces, the Army,

              Police, Carabiniere, Intelligence Services, and other special
0
0             units.   On the other hand   I destroyed the communist
0       1     revolutionary apparatus.     Taking all of their arms and
a       0-
c)            neutralizing their agents, I stopped Soviet KGB operations; and

o             Italy was safe to rebuild from a war - torn country.
C       Jr'        I realized now that my destruction of the Gestapo, SS and
C             stay - behind sabotage nets was a secondary operation.      Primary

C)            was the establishment of the forces of the law and order and the

C)            Oestruction of the threat to Italy's return into the family of

              free nations.   In short, I only held the line while the Italian
C)
              Government was reestablishing itself; and the shattered economy

C)            was coming to life.
C)                 During the time, I never gave this situation much thought.
0
              I did not plan for such an eventuality, but was only performing

              as an Army officer in charge of security.     My goal was the
C)            protection of Americans and their installations in Rome.     I never
C)
              fully realized that in performing this mission, within its
C)
              framework fell the establishment of the Italian security forces,

              which was necessary for the future and well - being of a great
(D            country --Italy.
C)      +71


C)                 My Italian colleagues and friends realized this and gave me

              credit for an accomplishment which more than likely was planned

              by some Power much greater than all of us.    Perhaps this is what

c)            Pope Pius XII was thanking me for, and the recognition he

c)            afforded me in repeated private audiences.    Now I know what the


    C
                                                                       Icifb
great Monsignor 0'Flaherty was thinking when he said, "I was a
                                                                          CD
chosen one;"     and why he embraced me like a child when i said,         CD
,,If I am a chosen one, why doesn't He let me beet you
                                                       playing            CD
golf?"
                                                                          CD
                                                                          C)
        During the course of the dinner, I turned to General              C)
De Pasquale and said, "General, you know that one of your prize           CD
pupils ended up on the United States White House Staff."       He
looked at me puzzled; and I put my hand on his shoulder and
                                                                          C)
added, "Don't you know who he was?    I am the one!   Before you and

General Agrifoglio, I never considered myself more than a pupil."          CD
                                                                           CD
This brought a round of applause, and he reached over. and took my
                                                                         • CD
hand.    Then .he said, "We always knew General Eisenhower would

recognize your talents, and you would be placed in a high

position."    I thought to myself, perhaps, perhaps not.   Was it
                                                                           CD
planned or an accident?    I do not know, but favor the latter.            C)
     Then, naturally, came my turn to speak.    I began, "Friends,         CD
such dear friends as you, made in trying times, last a lifetime;
                                                                           0
and so we are here.    I came to Rome as a boy, 28 years. old; I was

seared and fearful that I would not be able to restore order out

of chaos, and thought I had very little to work with, because our

combat forces had left Rome and were after the Germans fleeing

north.    I even challenged God, and in one of my weak moments, I

said, "Did you put me here as a joke?    How am I going to restore
                                                                         -c
law and order?"    Now, when I look back, these were the rantings

of an immature boy.    How could / go wrong when I had such

brilliant and brave men such as you at my side,    When I went on

raids for the .communist arms, two of my brave Italian Customs

guards always flanked me; and their Lieutenant was at my side.


                                                                               C
                 When the shooting began, he always stepped in front of me to

                 protect me.   I admonished him for this, but he would only smile.

                      "I should have known that there were men such as you and

                 them, in Italy.   At Cassino, I saw 800 of your student officers

                 killed in a German crossfire.     I stood up in the observation post
0                and yelled above the battle din, "Why, why, do they attaek in
0
                 such a manner?"   Their General, with tears in his eyes, said, 'To

        1t1,_    show your Army and the world that the Italian soldier is not

                 afraid to fight and die for his country.'"

                    "I was liaison officer with the Italian troops entrusted with

0                a sector of our line at Cassino.    They stopped furious German
0                attacks and held and threw them back in valiant counter attacks.

        et!      They were commended by British General Alexander for this action,

                 although at one time I thought they were going to kill me when

                 they taught me to drink grappa.    (They laughed at this.)"

                    "I still remember one day when I told General be Pasquale, My

                 job is to restore law and order and turn Rome back to you.        My

                 country's policy is to reestablish Italy into the family of free
0                nations.   My Army's policy and my personal policy are to restore
0
                 your service and, along with it, your pride and dignity.      I

0               intend to do this with or without your help.     I can never tell

                you how the Polipe, the Army, the Carabiniere, the Italian
0
                Intelligence, the Ministry of Interior, and even your great Prime

0               Apinister Abide De Gaspari responded.     With the support of my

                Commanding General, Major General Charles Dasher, and the British

                Brigadier Edwin Lowe, I was able to work without hindrance.        The

    0           result is apparent."

                     "As,I look back, you are free men in a free country.


    0
Friends, with the help of many who are no longer here, we won.

The Vatican is still here as a. symbol of Christianity to the

whole world.   Rome is still the "Eternal City," and Italy stands

tall in the family of free nations.     Gentlemen, we did it, and I
thank you for seeing fit to give me your Croce de Guerra for

military valor."   The dinner came to an end at this point with a

rousing ovation.

     During all this, my wife Eunice, the only woman in the

audience, as she had done many times before, patiently waited,

not understanding a word.   Years later, when I gave speeches

before the Rotary Club, Kiwanis, and the DeA.R., she always

remarked, "When you get on that podium, you are in your glory,

whether speaking in English or Italian."

     We went back to Foligno and enjoyed a pleasant vacation with

my long - time friend Ezio Rapanelli and his family.     We had a

pleasant trip home, to Innsbruck, Austria, Garmisch, Germany, and

then Munich and back home to Karlsruhe. Germany.

     However, I felt that I left an unsolved puzzle in Rome.      The

old coin I had picked up in the Roman ruins, looked at and then

threw it as far as I could back into the dust.    Also, the episode

when I was walking alone on one of' my nocturnal jaunts through

the deserted streets of Rome, and the manor shape stepped in

front of me.    When we entered Rome, among my duties was to

takeover all public buildings.   One building was Palazzo Venezia.

Oh the front was the balcony form which Mussolini harangued the

crowds.   I usedeto go up the back way to the large room from

which Mussolini's balcony extended,   /n one of the frescoes, I

recognized the form I saw that night.    I went back down the worn
                                                                                  vair


             marble steps, which showed the wear from the footsteps of Rome's

             great rulers throughout the ages, back to my office and reality

             across the street.

                  I dismissed both events as fantasy and would not bore the.

             render, except for the reaction of the Chief of Police of Italy,
0            Ferrari.   De was an astute and brilliant man and had been a
0            Supreme Court Judge.   I never told these tales to anyone but him.
0
             His reaction was never light,. but in a serious vein.
0                                                                    Although I

             Joked about the "Devil knife," he took it seriously. ' He always


0
        t] ,insisted I was,one of theirs, and I was sent here for a purpose.
0
0            I expected him to laugh at the coin episode and the mysterious
0
0
        )
        #5   night prowler.   Instead, he took it most seriously. I wondered,

             and still wonder, why?"

                         THE SECOND RETURN TO ROME,' 1974
LI                                                                             C)
                                                                               C)
             In 1974, fifteen years after the dinner and thirty years

     after I became the Chief Intelligence and Security of the Rome            C)
     Area Allied Command, my close friend Ezio Rapanelli wrote to me           C)
                                                                               C)
     and said his youngest son Anty was getting married.       He insisted
                                                                               C)
     I come to Italy and attend the wedding as a member of the family.
                                                                               0
     Naturally, I accepted the invitation; and it was a most                   CD
                                                                               C)
     delightful and happy event.     The reception was at the little
                                                                                C)
     Napoleon Fort near Rimini; and the wedding was held in a
                                                                                C)
     beautiful ancient Church.     There were no pews or seats; all                C)

     guests stood.    It was decorated in a sea of flowers and most'               C)
                                                                                   C)
     charming.    We returned to Foligno.   I mingled with his 1,500

     workers, and they were happy and contented.     Then after a series

     of dinners, Ezio and I took off for Sicily and Calabria on a

     business trip.    We drove, and I got a good feeling for the way of
                                                                                   C)
     life.    We stopped in many small towns.    Crime was not a serious           C)
     problem.    We often drove at night with no incidents.     Industry           C)

     was thriving in these two areas considered the poorest in Italy.

      Was this another legacy Of the foundation we had laid thirty                 C)
     years ago?    Marxism and socialism were only theories.    Italy had          C)

     become a free capitalistic Western Republic.     Upon our return to

     Foligno, I had another experience.     Ezio took me to a piazza

     paved with old, rounded stones.     When we got out of the car, I

     said, "Enzio, why did you bring me here?"     He said, "There is n

     good restaurant over here, and the family is coming."      I added,

     "Ezio, I don't like this place.     The elders of Foligno disgraced


                                                                                    C
themselves here by a shameful reception and humiliated themselves

in doing honor to a poisoning murderess. Did Lucrezia Borgia stay

at the inn where we are to dine?"      he asked, "How do you know?"

I responded, "I guess I rend it in some book."      I have a story to

tell, Ezio, which is Only for your ears. We are the some types --

a little crazy, some intelligence, some courage, and attached to

our families.     Trouble makers avoid us for some reason."

     "Ezio, I am getting drunk with this fine wine, and I guess

good wine at times creates illusions.      I have been in this

piazza.    I was a soldier.   I feel a great anger.    A powerful mun

had someone, in our family killed.     I had to get him.   I think I

did."     At this moment, the family came in; and our conversation

ended.

        The next morning I awoke with a hangover and reflected, was

it a dream?     The next day Ezio's man drove me to the airport near

Rome.    I went to the TWA center, changed my booking for the next

day, and checked my bag.      I took the bus to Rome and checked in

the hotel where I had lived.     I walked down Via Veneto, sat at      2


sidewalk cafe, and watched the people pass by.        I walked through

Villa Borghese to the old C.I.C. headquarters, then back to the

hotel.    I had dinner, then went to my room.

     I awoke at 1:00 a.m. and went out on a. nocturnal prowl.      I

headed for Via Sicilia.    It was quiet, deserted, and eerie -- one

of those nights when nothing moves and the hot, humid air seems

to press down on you.    I walked for about an hour.    Just before

3:00 a.m., I returned to the hotel.     I walked up to the desk

clerk and asked for my key.     The night clerk said, "Si, Senor

Colonello."
                                                                                ',P10

             I said, "How did you know I was a Colonel?   I never


ii   registered as so and never saw you before."     He answered, "A

     gentlemen was here about an hour ago, and he asked for you."           I

     didn't act surprised and said, "Did he leave a name, and what did

     he look like?"     He described a Professor of Archaeology at the

     University of Rome who had taught me the basis of life, which I

     wrote about in a previous chapter.    He described a man I knew

     thirty years ago, who must be 70 or 80 (since I was now 60); and

     in the clerk's eyes he appeared as in 1944.    The clerk said,

     "Senor, he left an envelope for you."

             194: Another fifteen years have passed, forty-five years

     since I became AC of S G-2 of the Rome Area Allied Command.        A

     few weeks ago, I telephoned my close friend Ezio RapanelIi.       He

     and his wife expect me to come to visit him at Foligno this

     summer.    I will go.   What is in store for me on this trip?     Will

     this be my last?    Only time will tell, and I don't fear any

     revelations, since they have already happened.

          The next morning, I was on my way home by TWA.     I had put

     the envelope, given to me by the hotel clerk, in my inner coat

     pocket.    I tdok it out and laid it on my lap, unopened, and began

     to reflect.    I thought back, my entire tour of duty in Rome had

     been, it seemed, a mystical, historical episode.     Whatever was in

     the envelope would only be a prologue of the past.     Was this

     envelope only another in the series of events that had happened

     in Italy, specifically in Rome, during the centuries.     Who

     brought the envelope?    Fifteen years ago, the Carabiniere General

     knew of my visit soon after I entered Italy through the Brenner

     Pass.    This time, I came into Italy and had to show my passport
                 to customs.    Was this envelope from my old Customs agents?       Was

                 it from my old friend, the Professor at the University of Rome?

                 Was the hotel clerk sleepy and gave a confused description?         Or

                 did I want to believe it was the mYsterious, brilliant Professor
0                of Archaeology and Criminology?      I remember one lesson which
0
                 shaped my future.      During one of the lessons, he showed me a
0   -z,
    P!
    !-
                 picture of Stalin and split it in half.      He put the opposite

    tria         sections together and said, "Notice the criminal countenance.
0
    1[1          These are the two faces of communism.      Don't ever trust them."       I

0                will never forget the brilliant dissertation on the basis of
0                life; the setting on a wall under a. starlight night on the Island

                 of Capri.    One of those clear nights when you can see millions of
0
                 stars, and the universe seems never ending.       I can still remember

0                when, naively, I asked, "Where did. you learn all of this?"        His

                 answer, "Does it matter?"     I answered, "No."

                      My mission in 198V will be to find out if he is still alive

0                or exists.    Perhaps I am biting off more than I can comprehend,

                 but I must try.    To me, he was always real.     He enjoyed
0
                 "gatherings" of young people and seemed to enjoy their simple
0
0                games, in which I participated, such as, the "Win, Lose or Draw
0                program on national TV.     I hope to answer this question by the

                 end of 1987, or perhaps I should leave" it like the famous story
                                                                       itoc Klôgt
                 with no end, what was behind the door, as told in 11 ,115gare "The

                 Lady or the 'Tiger."

                                  family tree.

                                  certificates, Communion and Confirmation data, etc.           One of the

                                  facts they Uncovered was that an ancestor of mine had been a
                                   military officer in the army of King of Naples and Sicily.              He

                                   commanded troops and must have been equivalent to a Colonel.                 He

                                   was born in Central Italy near Perugia. He migrated to Naples and

                                   was given a grant of land in Sicily.          They even found my family

                                   coat of arms.  They went back about five hundred years.
                                                                                             vAirrrefi 5 7,-Afg:5,
                                                            (ON 7-71.6 TWA Pr-Amg knofp yalt
                                        I leaned back in my seatj and I- remembered the criticism of

                                   my wife Eunice; she could never understand how as soon as a plane

                                   took off, I would fall asleep and she couldn't.           When I traveled
                                                                           •


                                   alone, I always used to request the stewardess to awake me if I

                                   began to snore.    I dozed off.

                                        Suddenly, I was in Foligno, Italy.         I was relaxing in the

                                   city with about 50 soldiers.       We were looking for a group of

                                   marauders who were terrorizing the Province of Romanga.             In mid


       00000000Q000000000000000
                                   afternoon, a rider came with the news that they had sacked and

                                   pillaged the town of Camerino, to the east.          It was a stifling,

                                   hot day; and we were lightly armored, both men and horses.              We

                                   quickly mounted and rode eastward.          I was on the great black

                                   horse, "Corso."    I was in the lead.        We approached a sharp curve

                                   in the hilly country.      An armed knight came around the curve.

                                   Less than thirty meters separated us.         (Like the wild west

                                   gunfighter, I had the sun at my back.)           Dr-saw=hks—coat.470t7,avras;

                                   I urged the swift "Corso" at full speed and the great horse

                                   responded.    Wh were almost on him before he saw me.          I had

                                   recognized his coat of arms.      This was the cruel murderer I had

                                   sworn to kill.    He had laughed at my challenge.         I was not more

                                   than five meters from him when he recognized my coat of arms.                 A

                                   look of terror swept over his face.         The great black horse

                                   "Corso"   and I had practiced this maneuver so often that it was a
natural for him.     He swerved to the left.    Because of the heat,
my enemy had his helmet off.     His horse, under the heavy, hot

armor, could not respond in time.     He tried to draw his sword,
but my right arm swung low and up and out.       With a snap of the
wrist, my stilleto flew through the air and went through his

neck.   Blood gushed from his mouth; his horse reared, and he

crashed to the ground.

     This all happened in a few seconds.       His favorite murder

weapon was a garrote.     Now his hands were on his own throat

trying to free himself of my knife.     His band of marauders came

into view; and when they saw their.leader on the ground, they

turned to flee;     but their tired, hot horses, with their heavy

loads, could not respond in time.     My swift, refreshed soldiers

were on them in a flash; none survived. . The murdering band was

no more.   Their leader, whose name struck terror in the

population, lay dead in the dust.     My men formed a circle.    I

said, "There is the devil's spawn; strip him and send him to his

Maker as he arrived on this earth -- naked!

     And so ended the reign of terror of the cruelest man of his

time "Michelotto."     He was buried in the side of the road, and

the horses passed over his grave; and no trace of him was found

to be recorded in history.

     Next, I found myself on a cold, rain - swept plain in

northern Spain.    I saw a large man on a huge chestnut horse

bearing down on me..    He was carrying a double - pointed lance.

He was raging like a madman.    I could hear him above the wind.       I

rode to meet him.     I crouched lbw over "Corso's " neck and told

him, "Swiftly, Caro" (precious one.)     As we drew near, my great
